iii
About This Report
Starting with the administration of President Donald Trump and continuing into the administration of Pres-
ident Joseph Biden, the United States has placed increasing restrictions on economic exchange with China. 
How does the U.S. business community view these policies, and does it broadly support increased efforts to 
counter problematic Chinese economic behavior? If not, how could the U.S. government implement policy 
to better incorporate business interests, address corporate concerns, and gain greater cooperation from the 
U.S. business community? In this report, we attempt to answer these questions that are central to whether 
the U.S. government has crafted an overall economic strategy or approach to China that is sustainable and 
feasible. The views of the U.S. business community on how the United States should deal with China are an 
overlooked and underappreciated topic. The United States may find it difficult to compete against China 
without support from the U.S. business community.
This report should be of value to the national security community and interested members of the general 
public—especially those with an interest in U.S.-China dynamics. The research was conducted during fiscal 
year 2020 and prior to the 2020 U.S. presidential election. 
Funding
Funding for this research was made possible by the independent research and development provisions of the 
RAND Corporation’s contracts for the operation of its U.S. Department of Defense federally funded research 
and development centers.

Summary
During its four years, the administration of President Donald Trump pursued an enhanced—and, in some 
ways, novel—set of policies to confront China over its economic policies, including China’s theft of technol -
ogy and intellectual property (IP), limitations to market access for U.S. businesses, and other anticompeti -
tive behaviors. How did the U.S. business community view the Trump administration’s economic policies 
focused on the People’s Republic of China (PRC), and did it broadly support increased U.S. efforts to coun -
ter problematic PRC economic behavior? If not, how could the U.S. government implement policy to better 
achieve policy goals and at the same time address corporate concerns? In this report we attempt to answer 
these questions that are central to whether the U.S. government has crafted an overall economic strategy or 
approach to China that is sustainable and feasible. The views of the business community on how the United 
States should deal with China are an overlooked and underappreciated topic, and the United States may find 
it difficult to compete against China without support from the business community.
This report analyzes how the U.S. business community—specifically, the manufacturing, technology, 
and financial sectors—viewed the Trump administration’s China policies through several different lenses: 
(1) from the perspective of individuals working in different industries, (2) from the perspective of different 
firms over time, and (3) from the perspective of specific industry sectors and subsectors. The analysis focuses 
on Trump administration actions toward China from 2017 through the fall of 2020. The RAND Corporation 
team concluded the majority of the research for this report in October 2020 and before the November 2020 
presidential elections.
National Survey of Views on China
We conducted a national survey of U.S. adults from March to April 2020 to assess individual-level views on 
China. We find that the U.S. public generally views China as both a military or security threat and an eco -
nomic opportunity (Table S.1). The U.S. public is generally supportive of restricting U.S. corporate products 
or services to China and boycotting problematic PRC products, suggesting a willingness to curb potential 
economic gains from China to protect U.S. interests and values. At the same time, the majority of U.S. survey 
respondents did not support limiting Chinese workers and students in the United States. This suggests that 
any U.S. government effort to limit PRC student and worker visas is likely to be met by public resistance. 
There is little evidence that individual views on China are linked to an industry of employment, although we 
did find some evidence to suggest that those in the financial sector were less likely to support limits on PRC 
nationals studying or working in the United States. 
Earnings Calls to Analyze Business “Sentiment” Toward China
We also examined how different industries and subsectors responded to the Trump administration’s actions 
against China by analyzing the transcripts of quarterly corporate earnings calls from companies in the Stan -
dard & Poor’s (S&P) 500 Index and the Russell 2000 Index. We assessed what the corporate earnings calls 
conveyed about the company’s views about China or its business prospects with or in China. Figure S.1 shows 
the results for the S&P 500 companies. 
We find that manufacturing and technology firms had earnings calls that voiced more concern with 
(had decreasing sentiment regarding) China-related operations from 2018 to late 2019. This suggests that 
manufacturing and technology firms may have faced more difficulties and costs from the policies the Trump

===== PDF_PAGE 6 =====

administration implemented with respect to China. In contrast, large financial companies listed in the S&P 
500 saw a dip in sentiment in early 2018 (compared with late 2017) and subsequent increase in positive sen-
timent from 2018 to mid-2020. This suggests that financial firms could have viewed the actions taken by 
the Trump administration as providing them with slightly more opportunities or benefits compared with 
costs. We do not find any significant difference between how large U.S. firms and small U.S. firms (based on

Summary
vii
the Russell 2000 Index) viewed China-related activities. Furthermore, we note that there are large standard 
deviations to our estimates, and so while the point estimates differ, there may have been little difference in 
sentiment by sector.
How Sectors Viewed the Trump Administration’s China Policies 
and Their Impact
Finally, we assessed how each sector viewed the Trump administration’s China policies and whether the poli-
cies had a generally positive or negative impact on the sector. Our research involved looking across different 
business functions and interviewing nearly 30 industry experts. We find that while companies and industry 
experts were broadly supportive of the Trump administration’s goals to level the playing field for American 
companies and counter problematic PRC practices, there were significant concerns about the unilateral and 
seemingly ad hoc tactics that the administration took with limited consultation with businesses. The manu -
facturing sector was negatively affected the most by the Trump administration’s actions while the technology 
sector—both on the hardware and software sides—also felt largely negative impacts. The effects of the Trump 
administration’s policies were more mixed (and overall neutral) with respect to the financial sector, and there 
is debate as to whether the Trump administration successfully encouraged China to accelerate the opening of 
its financial markets or if Beijing was already on track to do so to support its own domestic agenda anyway. 
Table S.2 summarizes the findings.

Recommendations
China is intertwined with the U.S. and global economies. The United States does not have unlimited leverage 
on its own, and any policy is likely to have costs. While the Trump administration’s policies can certainly be 
criticized, arriving at effective alternatives is not a simple task. With that in mind, our research suggests a 
number of recommendations for how the administration of President Joseph Biden could improve its ability 
to work with the U.S. business community to advance U.S. economic interests and counter problematic PRC 
economic behavior: 
•
 Ad
opt an economic strategy that balances efforts to counter China with investments in strengthen -
ing and sustaining U.S. economic and technological leadership. The Trump administration’s eco -

===== PDF_PAGE 8 =====
Bridging the Gap
viii
nomic approach to compete with China was largely focused on countering China via imposing uni -
lateral tariffs on Chinese trade, preventing Chinese control of sensitive U.S. technology, and limiting 
transfers of U.S. technology to China. The Trump administration sought to invest domestically to 
strengthen U.S. technology leadership in select areas, such as artificial intelligence and quantum phys -
ics, and not in basic science. There are a large number of measures the new U.S. administration could 
embrace to help the United States and U.S. companies compete with China. In general terms, for exam-
ple, the administration could increasing funding for science, technology, engineering, and mathematics 
education and research or provide tax incentives for companies to invest more in research and develop-
ment. More specifically, U.S. policy development could be directed toward providing new methods of 
skill building beyond traditional educational programs, exploring programs to improve labor market 
matching to make better use of human capital, and creating programs or facilities, such as shared design 
or production centers, to accelerate the diffusion of advanced manufacturing techniques. 
•
 Em
brace the full range of trade and economic tools to counter China and expand those tools. The 
new U.S. administration could also do more to employ a fuller range of tools to counter problematic 
PRC behavior. The United States, for example, could more fully embrace existing trade enforcement 
tools and reinvigorate the World Trade Organization’s trade dispute mechanism to help address Chi -
na’s unfair trade practices. The United States currently seizes counterfeit and pirated goods and has a 
process to block imports of items that use stolen IP; this could be broadened to block any import from 
a company that uses stolen IP. Other policies could include measures involving more reciprocal treat -
ment for market access, especially for Chinese investment in the United States. In combination, these 
tools could help U.S. companies compete on a more level playing field and obtain more market access 
in China without necessarily requiring U.S. companies to incur the costs of tariffs or limits on their 
activities in China. 
•
 Wo
rk with allies and partners to embrace multilateral measures against problematic PRC activi-
ties. The Biden administration should consult allies and partners prior to embracing major economic 
actions and, if possible, adopt multilateral instead of unilateral economic actions. Unilateral U.S. politi-
cal or economic pressure against problematic PRC economic behavior is unlikely to change that behav-
ior and may hurt U.S. businesses instead of benefiting them. Unilateral restrictions on economic activ -
ity with China also risk larger geopolitical ramifications beyond the U.S.-China relationship and could 
influence other countries to adopt similar protectionist measures to protect their manufacturing or 
technology sectors. This could challenge existing global economic rules that support open markets and 
free trade. The European Union, the United States, and their Western market-economy partners are the 
primary import markets for Chinese goods, and China relies on selling those goods for employment 
and growth. Accordingly, the combined Western economies have far more leverage together than any 
one of them individually, even the United States.
•
 Co
nsult U.S. business leaders regularly in advance of major policy changes. Business leaders have sig-
nificant information and experience dealing with the Chinese government and competing against Chi -
nese firms and can provide valuable input into potential major economic U.S. policy changes or actions 
targeting China. According to our interviews, this was one of the biggest gaps in Trump administration 
policy implementation. Even if U.S. corporate leaders disagree with a proposed policy change, consul -
tations and advance notice will allow them to better plan and adapt business operations accordingly. 
•
 Av
oid U.S. government actions that target the Chinese people. There is little public support in the 
United States for limiting PRC nationals from studying or working in the United States. Chinese stu -
dents and workers also contribute significantly to the U.S. economy and companies as valuable col -
leagues and business leaders.

===== PDF_PAGE 9 =====
Summary
ix
• Adopt an information strategy to better inform the public about the risks of, or problems associated 
w
ith, PRC products. Our survey results showed that when informed of the potential risks of a Chinese-
made telephone, a majority of the U.S. public declined to purchase the technology. This suggests that the 
United States could invest in efforts to publicize information regarding the operations and relationships 
of problematic Chinese companies and allow U.S. customers to make informed decisions on which 
products to purchase. Such information activities could be conducted in place of or in conjunction with 
U.S. government measures to limit the activities of select PRC companies.

CHAPTER ONE
Introduction
In the final weeks of the administration of President Donald Trump, the U.S. government pushed out a series 
of measures against Chinese companies. These included allowing U.S. authorities to delist Chinese and other 
foreign firms from U.S. stock exchanges if they do not comply with U.S. audit rules; banning U.S. firms from 
investing in Chinese companies linked to the People’s Liberation Army (PLA); restricting U.S. companies 
from engaging in transactions with select Chinese software companies; and adding more Chinese firms to 
the U.S. Department of Commerce (DOC) Entity List, which limits the ability of these firms to trade with 
U.S. companies.
1 These measures added to the already tense economic relationship between the two coun -
tries and built on the tariffs that the United States and the People’s Republic of China (PRC) have levied on 
each other since 2018—the “tariff tit-for-tat” that many experts call the U.S.-China trade war. 
Although the administration of President Joseph Biden acted immediately to roll back a number of domes-
tic and foreign policy measures set by the Trump administration, there was little movement on the economic 
side. The new U.S. administration initially largely maintained the previous one’s economic approach toward 
China.
2 This continued at least through September 2021. The Biden administration nominee for U.S. Trade 
Representative (USTR), Katherine Tai, pointed out the need to hold China accountable and appeared to share 
concerns the Trump administration had regarding problematic PRC economic behavior. When asked about 
specific measures the previous administration took—including whether the United States should reshore 
critical supply chains back to the United States—Tai commented that she sought to achieve “similar goals 
but in a more process-driven manner,” and the Biden administration would engage in a “holistic review” of 
China policy.
3 
This report analyzes the Trump administration’s economic approach toward China from the perspective 
of the U.S. business community. It addresses the central question of whether the U.S. business community 
supported and benefited from confronting China using the range of economic and other tools the Trump 
administration embraced. The views of the U.S. business community, including how the community may 
align with, resist, or circumvent U.S. government policies toward China, are relatively underexamined and 
less transparent, particularly since U.S. firms may be reluctant to publicly oppose or criticize U.S. (or Chi -
nese) government economic actions. Given the central role U.S. companies play in driving U.S. economic 
growth and technological leadership, support from the business community is critical for the U.S. govern -
ment if Washington seeks to develop an effective economic approach toward China. The Biden adminis -

tration and subsequent administrations would benefit from understanding where the business community

Causes and Consequences of the Trade War
In this report we discuss aspects of the trade war and related policies in great detail, but before doing so, we 
summarize the road to the trade war and its consequences. This will provide context for the actions that took 
place starting in 2017, detailed in Chapter Three.
China has experienced remarkable economic growth since it changed its economic policy to one of reform 
and opening up in the late 1970s. Between 1980 and 2020, China’s real gross domestic product (GDP) grew 
an annual average of almost 9.3 percent per year—the fastest growth rate among all major economies and the 
second fastest overall among economies with available data. And its per capita real GDP grew almost 8.3 per-
cent per year—the fastest rate among all economies with available data.
4
Much of this growth came from the same sources of growth behind the success of any major economy. 
These include more reliance on market mechanisms to allocate resources, increased education levels and 
better human capital, achieving greater efficiencies and larger markets through the use of imported inputs 
and a focus on exports, and increased levels of investment. China’s integration into the world economy 
achieved a milestone when the country joined the World Trade Organization (WTO) in 2001.
But there has been a darker side to this growth as well. Among these are technology and intellectual prop-
erty (IP) theft, markedly differential treatment of foreign companies in China versus Chinese companies 
in China including use of regulatory powers to favor domestic companies, and often opaque subsidies that 
provide advantages to Chinese companies worldwide. Regarding IP theft, one analysis found that China and 
Hong Kong account for 87 percent of identified counterfeit goods seized by the United States.
5 A separate 
estimate valued China’s theft of U.S. IP in the high tens of billions of dollars. 6 Regulatory actions are used to 
assist with this theft, such as the execution of antitrust actions or other regulatory investigations to threaten a 
company or by seizing technologies.
7 Subsidies can include direct subsidies, but also state financing at favor-
able rates, assistance with raising capital through mandated equity purchases, indirect subsidies through 
subsidies to inputs, barriers to competition by foreign firms, and use of IP stolen with state assistance.
8
These policies have provided some benefits to China. There has been significant technology upgrading of 
Chinese industry. Tracing specific cases of U.S. technology fueling this is difficult because companies doing 
business in China are generally reluctant to speak out publicly, but there have been revelations in court cases, 
such as accusations that the Chinese telecommunications firm Huawei stole internet router source code and 
4 Data are from World Bank, “World Development Indicators,” online database, last updated September 15, 2021. GDP 
growth variables are GDP (constant 2020 US$), series code NY.GDP.MKTP.KD; and GDP per capita (in constant 2010 dollars), 
series code NY.GDP.PCAP.KD. There were 128 economies with available data.
5 Commission on the Theft of American Intellectual Property, Update to the IP Commission Report: The Theft of American 
Intellectual Property; Reassessments of the Challenge and United States Policy , Washington, D.C.: National Bureau of Asian 
Research, 2017, p. 3.
6 Derek Scissors, “Some US-China Economic and Trade Facts,” American Enterprise Institute, October 2020, p. 5.
7 Lingling Wei and Bob Davis, “How China Systematically Pries Technology from U.S. Companies,” Wall Street Journal , 
September 26, 2018.
8 Jude Blanchette, Jonathan E. Hillman, Maesea McCalpin, and Mingda Qiu, Hidden Harbors: China’s State-Backed Shipping 
Industry, Washington, D.C.: Center for Strategic and International Studies, July 8, 2020.

Introduction
3
designs for network circuitry.9 By 2020 Huawei was the world’s biggest smartphone seller and was a leading 
information and communications technology networking company.10 
However, it is not clear how essential these malign behaviors have been to China’s growth. A variety of 
Asian countries have also grown rapidly following policies of export- and investment-led growth without 
the benefit of a large internal market, such as the one China has, and without the benefit of technology theft. 
Even if they have benefited China, the policies have also created international animosity. Furthermore, they 
have been overridden by China’s reliance on exports and investment, which has led to high levels of debt 
financing unproductive investments and to underconsumption, potentially stalling future growth.
11 Chinese 
leaders appear to be fully aware of the risks to the Chinese economy caused by unproductive investment and 
rising debt, but have as of mid-2021 been unable to solve this problem without causing large declines in GDP 
and increases in joblessness.
12
China’s economic policies have had both positive and negative consequences for its trade partners. The 
most positive consequence is that it has served as a new source of lower-cost inputs and products, leading to 
global efficiencies in production and increased average consumption. The efficiencies gained, and the large 
manufacturing economy in China, have kept companies there despite the difficulties of operating and of 
such malign activities as technology theft. Indeed, more than 95 percent of respondents to the 2021 U.S.-
China Business Council survey reported that their China operations were profitable, and most companies 
said their investments in China would remain the same or increase through 2021.
13
There have been negative consequences—some quite dramatic. Among these, the most noticeable and 
politically salient have been declines in U.S. manufacturing production and manufacturing employment 
and the concomitant rise in social problems as workers have had difficulty finding jobs that pay as well as 
the jobs they lost.
14 China’s strategy of high savings and suppression of consumption has also contributed to 
global imbalances in the form of large trade deficits, as measured by what is known as the current account, in 
some countries.
15 Finally, the theft of IP and the edge it has given Chinese companies in some cases has made 
global competition more difficult for U.S. and Western companies, with that difficulty arising from malign 
activities rather than standard business competition based on price and quality.
The United States has not previously taken such invasive steps as the sizable tariff increases, described 
in Chapter Three, to change China’s economic policies . Rather, it has used various economic measures and 
other forms of sanctions to either express displeasure at China’s actions or to change them. In particular, the 
United States has instituted sanctions and various kinds of economic pressure regarding the massacre of Chi-
nese citizens in Tiananmen Square in 1989, other human rights abuses, and weapons proliferation. It has also 
sanctioned Chinese individuals for weapons proliferation, terrorism, and narcotics trafficking.
16 In general,

10 “Huawei Tops Samsung as the World’s Biggest Smartphone Seller,” DW, July 30, 2020.

Bridging the Gap
4
sanctions aimed at human rights issues have not achieved their goals, whereas sanctions aimed at weapons 
proliferation have sometimes had positive effect.17
The wave of policies instituted since 2017 is more directly aimed at changing Chinese economic policy. 
These policies, and the Phase One trade deal signed on January 15, 2020, are discussed in Chapter Three. 
Here it is sufficient to note that the overall economic consequences of these policies have been negative for the 
United States. For example, one study has found that that tariffs instituted in 2018 and 2019 ended up being 
paid largely by U.S. importers and consumers, raising costs in the U.S. economy.
18 Another analysis found 
the tariffs cost U.S. exporters on average $900 per worker and ended up lowering export growth by making 
imported inputs more expensive.
19
The fact that most of these policies have been retained through September 2021, despite their costs, sug -
gests that policymakers either find value in them despite the costs or that they are uncertain of other policies 
they can adopt to achieve the same goals. However, many of these costs have fallen on businesses that either 
import from, sell to, or invest in China. Their views and the actions they can take will be relevant to the suc -
cess of these policies and the development of alternative policies.
Views from the U.S. Business Community
This report analyzes how the U.S. business community—specifically the manufacturing, technology, and 
financial sectors—viewed the Trump administration’s China policies through several different lenses: (1) 
from the perspective of individuals, (2) from the perspective of representatives of different firms over time, 
and (3) from the perspective of the consequences for specific industry sectors and subsectors. 
Our analysis of business community views is based on Trump administration actions toward China from 
2017 to the fall of 2020. The RAND Corporation team concluded the majority of the research for this report 
in October 2020 and prior to the November presidential elections in hopes of informing the subsequent 
administration regarding economic policies toward China without biasing our study based on the outcome 
of the election.
20 
We conducted a national survey of U.S. adults from March to April 2020 to assess individual-level views 
on China. We find that the U.S. public generally views China as a military or security threat and an economic 
opportunity. The public is generally supportive of restricting U.S. corporate products or services to China 
and boycotting problematic PRC products, suggesting a willingness to curb potential economic gains from 
China to protect U.S. interests and values. At the same time, the majority of U.S. survey respondents did not 
support limiting Chinese workers and students in the United States. This suggests that any U.S. government 
effort to limit PRC student and worker visas is likely to be met by public resistance or at least skepticism. 
There is little evidence that individual views on China are linked to a person’s industry of employment, 
17 Jing-dong Yuan, “Assessing Chinese Nonproliferation Policy: Progress, Problems and Issues for the United States,” Octo -
ber 12, 2001, posted January 4, 2002, by the Middlebury Institute of International Studies at Monterey. 
18 Mary Amiti, Stephen J. Redding, and David E. Weinstein, “Who’s Paying for the U.S. Tariffs?” Cambridge, Mass.: National 
Bureau of Economic Research, NBER Working Paper 26610, January 2020.

most of the concerns and perspectives of the U.S. business community.

Introduction
5
although we did find some evidence to suggest that those in the financial sector were less likely to support 
limits on PRC nationals studying or working in the United States. 
We also examined how different industries and subsectors responded to the Trump administration’s 
actions against China by analyzing the transcripts of quarterly corporate earnings calls and what these con -
veyed about a company’s views regarding China or its business prospects with or in China. Although large 
standard deviations in our statistical analysis make it difficult to assess our results with confidence, we find 
that manufacturing and technology firms had earnings calls that voiced more concern (in technical terms, 
had decreasing sentiment) about their China-related operations from 2018 to late 2019. This suggests that 
manufacturing and technology firms may have faced more difficulties and costs from the policies the Trump 
administration implemented with respect to China. In contrast, large financial companies listed in the Stan -
dard & Poor’s (S&P) 500 saw a dip in sentiment in early 2018 (compared with late 2017) but a subsequent 
increase in positive sentiment from 2018 to mid-2020. This suggests that financial firms could have viewed 
the actions taken by the Trump administration—or China’s reaction—as providing them with slightly more 
opportunities or benefits compared with costs. We do not find any statistically significant difference between 
how large and small U.S. firms (based on the Russell 2000 Index) viewed China-related activities. 
Finally, we assessed how each sector viewed the Trump administration’s China policies and whether the 
policies had a generally positive or negative influence on the sector. Our research involved looking across 
different business functions and interviewing nearly 30 industry experts. We found that while companies 
and industry experts were broadly supportive of the Trump administration’s goals to level the playing field 
for American companies and counter problematic PRC practices, there were significant concerns about the 
unilateral and seemingly ad hoc tactics the administration took for limited consultation with businesses. 
The manufacturing sector was negatively affected the most by the Trump administration’s actions, while the 
technology sector—both on the hardware and software side—also felt largely negative effects. The effects of 
the Trump administration policies were more mixed (and overall neutral) with respect to the financial sector, 
and there is debate as to whether the Trump administration successfully encouraged China to accelerate the 
opening of its financial markets or if Beijing was already on track to do so to support its own domestic agenda 
anyway. 
Road Map
This report is divided into nine chapters. We first discuss our research methodology in Chapter Two. Chap -
ter Three reviews the Trump administration’s policies to counter China and Chinese responses. Chapter Four 
details how we conducted our national survey of adults in the spring of 2020 and what our survey find -
ings showed about U.S. public attitudes toward China. Chapter Five presents our analysis of how companies 
viewed China by examining quarterly corporate earnings calls from hundreds of companies from the first 
quarter (Q1) of 2017 to the second quarter (Q2) of 2020. The following three chapters examine general trends 
and consequences of the Trump administration’s China policies on the manufacturing sector (Chapter Six), 
technology sector (Chapter Seven), and financial sector (Chapter Eight). Chapter Nine concludes by provid -
ing recommendations for the U.S. government and business community. Five appendixes provide further 
technical details.

CHAPTER TWO
Research Methodology and Approach
This report uses several methods to assess how the U.S. business community reacted to the Trump admin -
istration’s approach to countering problematic PRC economic practices. This chapter first defines what a 
U.S. business is and then provides an overview of how the subsequent chapters use survey results, analysis of 
U.S. corporate earnings calls, interviews with corporate leaders, and qualitative analysis of publicly available 
information to understand business community views.
Definition of the U.S. Business Community
To analyze views of the U.S. business community, it is important to first define what the community includes. 
The DOC defines a U.S. business or U.S. entity as 
a firm incorporated in the United States (or an unincorporated U.S. firm with its principal place of business 
in the United States) that is controlled by U.S. citizens or by another U.S. entity. An entity is not a U.S. entity 
if 50 percent plus one share of its stock (if a corporation, or a similar ownership interest of an unincorpo -
rated entity) is controlled, directly or indirectly, by non-U.S. citizens or non-U.S. entities.
1
The DOC also notes that “an entity is not a U.S. entity if more than 50 percent of its Board of Directors 
or membership is made up of non-U.S. citizens.” 2 The Internal Revenue Service attempts to further clarify 
things by noting that a “United States person” is a partnership or a corporation “created or organized in the 
United States or under the law of the United States or of any State, or the District of Columbia,” and “any 
estate or trust other than a foreign estate or foreign trust.”
3 The U.S. Department of the Treasury’s Office of 
Investment Security uses a broader definition, defining a U.S. business as “any entity, irrespective of nation -
ality of the persons that control it, engaged in interstate commerce in the United States”; it provides examples 
of what counts as U.S. businesses and includes subsidiaries of hypothetical Corporation A that is “organized 
under the laws of a foreign state and is wholly owned and controlled by a foreign national” as long as the sub-
sidiaries engage in interstate commerce in the United States.
4 Corporation A, however, would not count as a 
U.S. business. 
Although there is some variation in how different U.S. government agencies define a U.S. business, we 
draw from the underlying, basic agreement and define a U.S. business as a company with headquarters in the 
United States that conducts business in the United States.

Methodology for Assessing U.S. Business Sector Views
Several things make it difficult to assess where the U.S. business community stands with respect to intensi -
fied U.S.-China competition. As displayed in Figure 2.1, U.S. companies might obtain mixed benefits from 
the range of geopolitical and economic measures the Trump administration took against China. Some sec -
tors may benefit, while others may suffer losses. It also possible that U.S. businesses may see little benefit in 
publicly sharing views regarding U.S. government measures. They may also believe that they can navigate or 
find ways to circumvent U.S. policies. 
Focus on Three Key Business Sectors
To account for the possibility that different types of U.S. firms might have varied views of the Trump admin-
istration’s policies toward China, our report divides the U.S. business community into sectors. We focus on 
three critical sectors: manufacturing, technology, and finance. We have selected the manufacturing sector 
because President Trump campaigned on the promise to rebuild American factories and restore U.S. manu -
facturing.

Individual-Level Analysis: Survey of Public Views of China
Chapter Four begins by examining the business community through individuals and their perspectives on 
China. Individuals are the executives and employees of U.S. firms. Aggregating individual attitudes toward 
China by business occupation provides a way to examine if there are detectable differences in views based 
on economic characteristics, including what industry people work in. How the majority of people within a 
sector regard China could drive whether the sector supports more U.S. measures to confront problematic 
PRC behavior.
For this analysis, we commissioned a national public opinion survey of U.S. adults and examined their 
attitudes to identify factors that may be driving individual perceptions of China. Our survey included four 
questions that focused on the intersection and tensions between views of China as a security threat or oppor-

Firm- and Sector-Level Analysis: Earnings Calls Analysis and 
Sector-Wide Trends
In addition to individual-level analysis, we engaged in firm- and sector-level analysis in two different ways: 
(1) through conducting sentiment analysis on quarterly corporate earnings calls and (2) by assessing sector 
wide trends complemented by interviews with executives and experts from each sector. 
Sentiment Analysis of Corporate Earnings calls
Chapter Five engages in sentiment analysis of publicly available earnings calls to examine if U.S. companies 
have more positive or negative views of China-related developments. We downloaded all quarterly earnings 
calls from selected companies from Q1 2017 to the Q2 2020. For each earnings call, we assessed every sen -
tence in which the term China or three related terms ( geopolitical, tariff, or trade war) appeared and coded 
for whether the sentence conveyed a positive or negative sentiment. A more positive sentiment score suggests 
that a firm is relatively optimistic about its transactions or opportunities related to China, whereas a more 
negative sentiment reflects concern or pessimism with regard to the company’s China-related operations.
We selected U.S. companies from the S&P 500 Index and the Russell 2000 Index. The S&P 500 Index 
includes the 500 largest U.S. publicly traded companies; S&P 500 companies have a median total market 
capitalization of $26 billion, and the company with the smallest total market capitalization included in the 
index is valued at $3 billion.
13 In order to capture the possibility that there may be differences in how firms 
view U.S. economic measures against China depending on their size—whether they are large global, multi -
national firms or small and medium sized enterprises—we also included a sample of the smallest firms by 
market capitalization from the Russell 2000 Index. The median market capitalization of firms on the Russell 
2000 Index is $0.9 billion, significantly less than the median for firms from S&P 500.
14
For both indexes, we categorized U.S. companies into our three sectors based on the definitions we pro -
vided above and using each company’s Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS) classification. 15 The 
GICS codes do not map perfectly with the NAICS codes, and we include four GICS codes as manufactur -
ing (the consumer discretionary sector, consumer staples, industrials, and materials), two GICS subsectors 
for technology (information technology [IT] and communications), and one GICS subsector for finance 
(financials).
16

sectors, respectively. In each chapter, we examine how U.S. businesses responded to U.S. or PRC measures 
(discussed in Chapter Three) by looking at changes across five different business functions: operations (par -
ticularly supply chain management), sales and marketing, finance, research and development (R&D), and

We draw from a variety of sources for our research. Where possible, we use existing U.S. government sta-
tistics for the three sectors. When such sector-wide U.S. government statistics are not available, we use S&P 
500 companies to proxy for their broader sector at large, as a great deal of information is available for those 
companies (e.g., financial statements and public statements). Because S&P 500 companies include the largest 
U.S. firms, these firms are likely to have more influence on U.S. government policy than smaller firms with 
fewer resources for lobbying or smaller companies that employ fewer workers.
17 
Finally, we complement publicly available information on business sectors and specific companies with 
interviews with U.S. government officials, U.S. industry and company representatives (representatives from 
business associations and from individual companies), and academics and industry experts. We conducted 
30 such interviews, of which eight represented the manufacturing sector, seven represented the technology 
sector, nine represented the financial sector, and six were cross-sector. Most of these interviews were of single 
people, although one of the manufacturing interviews included two people and one of the finance interviews 
included three people. We interviewed each person or organization only once, and identify their remarks in a 
footnote by the date of the interview. This protects their identities but still provides the reader with informa-
tion about the range of inputs and beliefs communicated.
17 Our interviews with business executives leading small firms suggest that smaller firms are generally more resource and 
personnel constrained. While some may join larger industry-wide associations, others may not see the utility of investing 
significant resources in lobbying or trying to change U.S. government policy. Some smaller firm executives doubt that even if 
they lobbied the U.S. government, the government would place much weight on concerns of small firms, because those firms 
employ fewer workers or have less revenue, profit, or sales than larger firms and thus less influence over the economy.

CHAPTER THREE
The U.S. Approach Toward China During the 
Trump Administration
Following the establishment of diplomatic relations between the United States and the PRC in 1979, the 
bilateral economic relationship has grown in size and complexity as China has risen to become the world’s 
second-largest economy.
1 Facilitated by U.S. support for China’s entry into the WTO in 2001, each country 
became the other’s largest single-nation trading partner until the trade war led to a sharp decline in bilateral 
trade in 2019.
2 Along with trade, foreign direct investment (FDI), research, tourism, education, and other 
linkages also connect the U.S. and Chinese economies. Since 1979, U.S. businesses have generally viewed 
strong and expanding U.S.-China bilateral ties as beneficial to their bottom line.
3 Some U.S. businesses have 
pursued operating in China or developing Chinese supply chains to take advantage of low labor costs and 
China’s growing manufacturing capacity, while some have sought to expand revenues and market share by 
attracting consumers in the world’s most populous nation. 
In recent years, U.S. policymakers have become increasingly concerned with assertive and problematic 
Chinese behavior, manifesting in the Trump administration’s characterization of China as a “revisionist 
power” with which the United States is engaged in strategic competition in the political, economic, and secu-
rity domains.
4 This view is fueled by the perception that China has not played by the rules upheld by interna-
tional trade and financial organizations and has taken actions that could undermine the U.S. economy and 
the international economic system as it now operates. 
The discussion in Chapter One on causes and consequences of the trade war details some of the ways 
in which China has broken generally accepted international rules, but some of China’s actions that have 
stayed within the rules have also fueled international displeasure. One example is China’s participation in 
the WTO. When China joined, other members were allowed to treat it not as a market economy for purposes 
of certain actions to counter impermissible trade actions. China insisted that its accession protocol required 
all members to treat it as a market economy by December 11, 2016, and this would have made it more diffi -
1 This assessment is based on market exchange rates. Using purchasing power parity to estimate China’s economy results in 
China being the world’s largest economy. For more on this topic, see Tim Callen, “Purchasing Power Parity: Weights Matter,”

high levels of state-directed economic activity and subsidies in the Chinese economy, there was a case to be 
made that China was not a market economy, and the European Union (EU), Japan, and the United States all 
objected to the change of China’s status. China entered WTO dispute resolution, but withdrew from the case 
when it appeared that the ruling would be strongly against its position.
6 
U.S. policymakers have linked bilateral economic issues to broader national security arguments about the 
need to compete with China at a whole-of-government, or even whole-of-society, level. During the Trump 
administration, U.S. policymakers took a number of actions with the stated goal of strengthening economic 
competitiveness vis-à-vis China and bringing it into conformance with international rules. China has coun -
tered some of these actions through both direct and indirect responses.
The following sections summarize the Trump administration’s overall approach and economic policy 
toward China, and China’s responses to increased competition with the United States. These sections outline 
the range of U.S. and PRC efforts and activities that affect U.S. businesses, particularly U.S. businesses oper -
ating in China or dependent on trade with China.
China as a Long-Term Strategic Competitor
Released in December 2017, the Trump administration’s National Security Strategy (NSS) stated that eco -
nomic security is a key element of U.S. overall national security and identified China as a long-term strategic 
competitor due in part to its activities in the economic domain.
7 President Trump noted in his remarks on 
the NSS that “a nation that does not protect prosperity at home cannot protect its interests abroad,” 8 point-
ing to “past . . . 
 fa
ilures” that he characterized as unfavorable trade agreements that had sent American jobs 
overseas. In the NSS, U.S. leaders framed the challenge as “growing political, economic, and military compe-
titions we face around the world.”9 To compete more effectively with China, the NSS prescribed a change in 
the U.S. approach that involved “upgrad[ing] our political and economic instruments to operate across these 
environments.”
10 The U.S. Department of Defense’s (DOD’s) unclassified summary of the classified National 
Defense Strategy, released in January 2018, similarly reflects the emphasis on its interconnected approach to 
economic and national security.
11
Senior Trump administration officials have given various rationales for embracing whole-of-government 
competition with China. First, China is working to reshape the international order in its favor by eroding

explicit in the 2017 NSS and the Trump administration invoked national security arguments to its employment of tariffs and

The U.S. Approach Toward China During the Trump Administration
15
international institutions, coercing neighbors, and leveraging economic pressure to realize political objec -
tives vis-à-vis other countries. Second, China has long seen itself in competition with the United States and 
has steadily worked to erode U.S. advantages, including using influence operations to target U.S. citizens 
and organizations. Third, China takes a whole-of-nation approach to competition with the United States, 
including large-scale technology theft and state support that both coerces and undercuts U.S. businesses. 
Fourth, China fails to abide by international commitments, including its WTO obligations. Fifth, Chinese 
laws, regulations, and practices erode the privacy of Americans’ personal information. Sixth, Chinese Com -
munist Party leaders have directed large-scale human rights abuses in Xinjiang; curtailed Hong Kong politi -
cal, economic, and other freedoms; and increased multifaceted pressure on Taiwan.
12 DOD, U.S. Department 
of State, and White House reports covering U.S. policy toward China have raised these concerns. 13 
In 2020 the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic further reinforced the Trump administra -
tion’s view of China as a problematic global player. Senior U.S. officials criticized Chinese leaders’ efforts 
to cover up the outbreak and accused China of hoarding personal protective equipment, leading to global 
shortages, and shipping excess equipment to other countries for diplomatic gains. Then–Secretary of State 
Michael R. Pompeo, for example, repeatedly blamed China for exploiting the international community’s pre-
occupation with the pandemic to engage in provocative behavior against Hong Kong, against Taiwan, or in 
the South China Sea.
14 
These views of China are certainly not universal, and others either add more context or disagree. One 
in-depth study of China’s grand strategy found that China’s main aims throughout its modern history were 
not to reshape the international order but to restore and maintain the nation’s territorial integrity and keep 
any other power from dominating the Asia-Pacific region. More recently, it has aimed at so-called national 
rejuvenation in the form of a wealthy, strong, civilized, and harmonious nation. Furthermore, China’s lead -
ers believe that the United States is the disruptive partner in that it intends to cause the overthrow of rule 
by the Chinese Communist Party.
15 These goals do not necessarily contradict U.S. views, but they certainly 
emphasize different goals.
Others go further and disagree with the U.S. assessment. There appears to be agreement that China’s 
negative behavior has contributed to the deterioration of relations. Despite that, one China expert has noted 
that the idea that China wants to “build a revisionist, Sino-centric world order fundamentally incompatible

U.S. Policy to Counter PRC Economic “Aggression”
Although the Trump administration did not have one main publicly available document that comprehen -
sively laid out its economic strategy with regard to China, three public documents (the 2017 NSS, a 2018 
White House report on Chinese technology transfer, and a 2020 White House report to Congress) outlined 
the administration’s approach on countering what it identified as PRC economic aggression. The 2017 NSS 
described Chinese “economic aggression” as problematic PRC behaviors, including “dumping, discrimina -
tory non-tariff barriers, forced technology transfers, non-economic capacity, industrial subsidies, and other 
support from governments and state-owned enterprises.”
17 A 2018 report by the White House Office of Trade 
and Manufacturing Policy further specified six types of Chinese activities intended to 
1. pr
otect China’s home market from imports and competition, including tariff and nontariff barriers 
and regulatory issues
2. ex
pand China’s share of global markets via state support of Chinese firms to help them gain market 
share both in the Chinese domestic market and globally
3. se
cure and control core natural resources globally through nontransparent or financially unsus -
tainable lending (lending that might not be justified on strict business principles) and other practices
4. do
minate traditional manufacturing industries such as shipbuilding, automobile production, 
appliance manufacturing, and computer hardware manufacturing
5. ac
quire key technologies and IP from other countries , including the United States
6. ca
pture emerging high-technology industries that will drive future growth , including advance -
ments in the defense industry.18
A May 2020 White House report to Congress defined the Trump administration’s five specific economic 
approaches to China. First, the United States would enforce the so-called Phase One trade deal, signed in

17
January 2020, including increasing U.S. exports to China. Second, the United States would levy tariffs until 
both sides reach a Phase Two deal that addresses PRC technology transfer and IP theft. Third, the admin -
istration would seek to reduce the effects of Chinese subsidies and overcapacity by protecting strategic 
U.S. industries, including the manufacture of steel and aluminum; filing WTO cases against China; and 
harnessing existing antidumping and countervailing duties laws. Fourth, it would undertake domesti -
cally focused actions such as tax reform, deregulation, and promoting innovation in such sectors as 
fifth-generation (5G) telecommunications technology. Finally, the United States would work with allies 
and partners to adopt similar standards and policies. The report also mentioned the need to protect the 
American consumer from counterfeit and substandard products made in China and noted the admin -
istration’s focus on partnering with Congress, the private sector, academia, and other actors to better 
compete with China.
19
In addition to these approaches, and accelerated by the COVID-19 pandemic, senior Trump administra -
tion officials in 2020 raised the option of reshoring U.S. businesses and pursuing further economic decou -
pling between the United States and China. Some argued for the need to do so because U.S. firms are not 
allowed to “participate and compete on a fair basis” in China’s economy.
20 Others, such as Ellen Lord, then–
undersecretary of defense for acquisition and sustainment, said that amid the COVID-19 pandemic the DOD 
would like to “reshore as much as possible” after realizing its supply chain vulnerabilities, particularly for the 
supply of microelectronics and rare earth minerals.
21 
Nested under the Trump administration’s broader economic approach to China were specific goals for 
different sectors. Priorities for three key sectors are worth highlighting for this report: 
• Ma
nufacturing: Trump administration priorities were embedded within the administration’s broader 
strategy of “rejuvenating” the U.S. economy via job creation, advancing “free, fair, and reciprocal” 
trade relationships, and improving supply chain resiliency and expanding the defense industrial base.
22 
China-specific priorities included addressing the U.S.-China trade imbalance by expanding market 
opportunities for U.S. goods, countering Chinese dumping and other nontariff barriers, leveraging 
international institutions such as the WTO to enforce commitments that Beijing has previously agreed 
to, and lessening the impact of Chinese industrial subsidies on the U.S. economy.
•
 Te
chnology: The Trump administration focused on preserving U.S. “technological advantage” and pro-
tecting the U.S. economy “from competitors who unfairly acquire our intellectual property.” NSS focus 
areas included supporting U.S. leadership in emerging technologies and strengthening the National 
Security Innovation Base. China-related policy efforts include reducing technology transfer to China, 
restricting Chinese investment in U.S. firms working on sensitive or leading technologies, curtailing 
U.S. university and other research partnerships with the Chinese military or affiliated institutions, and 
protecting both commercial and public digital infrastructure from cyberexploitation.
23

• Finance: U .S. policy priorities for this sector centered on increasing market access.24 China has a largely

to reduce barriers to entry for U.S. firms into Chinese financial markets, including easing capital con -
trols and restrictions on foreign investment in Chinese companies. The administration also sought to 
improve the transparency of Chinese firms listed on U.S. stock exchanges. 26 
Major Lines of Efforts to Counter PRC Economic Behavior
Following investigations into specific cases in 2017, in early 2018 the administration began a series of trade 
actions, including raising tariffs on washing machines and solar panels and “considering recommendations 
on import restrictions for steel and aluminum, plus other trade sanctions against China over alleged intel -
lectual property theft.”
27 The DOC, in its so-called Section 232 reports, blamed China for global overcapacity 
of both steel and aluminum, finding that “the quantities and circumstances of steel and aluminum imports 
‘threaten to impair the national security,’ as defined by Section 232.”
28 Beijing itself launched a probe into 
imports of sorghum from the United States.29 In March 2018 the Trump administration announced a 10 per-
cent tariff on aluminum and a 25 percent tariff on steel products from China and other countries, “based on 
‘national security’ justifications under Section 232 of the Trade Act of 1962.” China countered by increasing 
tariffs on $3 billion in U.S. goods from 15 to 25 percent, and both countries additionally filed complaints 
against each other with the WTO.
30 Additional Section 232 investigations continued through 2020.
In August 2017 President Trump issued a memorandum stating that “China has implemented laws, poli -
cies, and practices and has taken actions related to intellectual property, innovation, and technology that may 
encourage or require the transfer of American technology and intellectual property to enterprises in China 
or that may otherwise negatively affect American economic interests.”

The tariffs initiated a round of over a dozen negotiations in 2018 and 2019, both to negotiate removing the

PRC Government Responses to the U.S. Approach to Competition
In addition to reviewing the Trump administration’s measures against China, it is important to also briefly 
examine Chinese reactions and measures China took against the United States economically. China responded 
to U.S. economic actions through a combination of carrots and sticks. Table 3.3 summarizes the PRC “sticks” 
(direct and indirect retaliation in response to U.S. actions) along with targeted “carrots” to incentivize the 
Trump administration, specific U.S. sectors, and individual firms to pursue greater cooperation with China 
(or Chinese firms) and reduce U.S. penalties or other negative actions against the Chinese government, enti-
ties, or individuals. Many of these measures, such as tariffs on U.S. goods, affected U.S. companies directly.
Summary
The Trump administration’s and the U.S. government’s response to problematic PRC economic practices 
involved numerous lines of effort. These included new laws, more aggressive use of existing laws, and actions 
that in some ways broke precedent in dealing with trade disagreements, particularly the 25 percent tariffs in 
response to the Section 301 investigation. China retaliated with its own measures, some reciprocal but others 
through use of authorities, powers, and methods available to it as part of its government-involved economic 
system, such as subsidies. While these actions were put in place by governments, the entities truly affected 
were the millions of businesses that trade and invest globally. In the next five chapters we turn to how busi -
nesses and their employees viewed and reacted to these policy changes.

CHAPTER FOUR
Individual-Level Analysis: Survey of U.S. Public 
Views of China 
One way to assess how U.S. business sectors view competition with China is by aggregating the views of indi-
viduals working in those sectors. Individuals are the leaders and employees of U.S. businesses. Individuals 
may support or oppose U.S. government policies based on how policies affect their companies, which in turn 
could improve or diminish their salaries and employment prospects. It is also possible for people to possess or 
develop varied views of China (or Chinese people) based on whether their jobs bring them into close contact 
with China (or Chinese people). This chapter analyzes four questions that we submitted to a nationally repre-
sentative U.S. survey to understand how American individuals view China and how they view three potential 
U.S. economic policy measures against China.
Method
We utilized the RAND American Life Panel (ALP), “a nationally representative, probability-based panel 
of respondents aged 18 and older who are regularly interviewed over the Internet for research purposes” to 
survey the U.S. public.
1 We used four questions to understand whether respondents view China as a threat 
or opportunity and assess their support for three measures the United States (or U.S. public) could adopt to 
engage in (or support) taking a tougher stance against China. The four survey questions are as follows:
Question 1. Do you see China as a security or economic threat to or opportunity for the United States?
Question 2. Should the U.S. government stop U.S. companies from providing services or goods to Chinese 
actors that undermine U.S. interests or values even if it weakens the U.S. economy? (Example: The U.S. gov-
ernment should stop U.S. companies from selling to Chinese companies that provide technology to censor 
the Chinese internet.)
Question 3. Foreign workers and students are important contributors to U.S. innovation, but a small por -
tion may illegally take innovation back to their home country, and others may return to their home country 
with new skills and compete against the United States. Should the United States limit Chinese workers and 
students even if it weakens the U.S. economy because of less innovation?
Question 4. The U.S. government has said that Huawei, a Chinese telephone company, could be collecting 
user information and intelligence on behalf of the Chinese military. Huawei has disputed such allegations, 
and many countries use Huawei phones. Are you willing to buy a phone produced by a Chinese technology 
company with potential links to the Chinese military?
1 For more information on the ALP, see RAND Corporation, “RAND American Life Panel,” webpage, undated. The questions 
were included in an ALP omnibus survey. The final compilation of questions, including these specific study questions, were 
submitted to RAND’s Human Subjects Protection Committee (HSPC) and approved under the master ALP HSPC protocol as 
appropriate for fielding. The master protocol includes an approved annual consent protocol and data safeguarding procedures.

===== PDF_PAGE 40 =====
Bridging the Gap
26
In addition to the four questions we submitted, the survey asked respondents for basic demographic vari-
ables such as race, education, gender, age, marital status, and income. 
The survey took place between March 3, 2020, and April 24, 2020; notably, this was at an important stage 
in the COVID-19 pandemic. On March 13, 2020, President Trump declared COVID-19 to be a national emer-
gency.2 Then, on March 19, California became the first state to issue a stay-at-home order, followed by 43 
states and the District of Columbia through April 7.3 The majority of responses were recorded within the first 
few days of the survey taking place, before the national emergency was declared and the stay-at-home orders 
were issued. RAND sent 3,403 invitations to potential participants. We received 2,488 completed responses, 
for a response rate of 73.1 percent.
To assess how individuals within different business sectors view China and potential U.S. measures against 
China, we created an industry variable based on reported occupation to capture those working in the manu -
facturing, technology, and financial sectors. We used the existing 22 occupation categories in the survey and 
allocated each occupation to a sector in line with the sector definitions in Chapter Two. Table A.1 in Appen-
dix A displays how we grouped different ALP occupation categories into our three sectors. This allocation 
is not wholly satisfactory, as many occupations could be carried on in any of the industries. Accordingly, 
this may introduce error into our analysis, as the relationship between specific occupations and specific 
industries is not one-to-one. However, we provide the results for two reasons: first, this report is aimed at 
understanding sectoral views and effects, and so we believe it is worth attempting to use the individual-level 
analysis for this purpose; and second, the weakness of the results provides support for using the other meth -
ods that appear in subsequent chapters. 
To isolate the impact of industry on individual views, we included the available demographic factors as 
control variables. We also created a variable to account for political affiliation based on the individual’s voter 
intent in the 2020 election. Table A.2 in Appendix A provides the summary statistics of all the demographic 
variables we included in our analysis. 
In the following sections we provide baseline statistics about the respondents who answered each ques -
tion. We then use statistical analysis (logistic regression) to estimate the determinants of participant views 
on China and participant support for U.S. measures against China, displaying results based on population 
weights.
4 Tables A.3 and A.4 in Appendix A provide full results.
Findings
We explore the findings of the survey first by respondents’ views on China and then by their views on three 
different potential U.S. economic actions.
2 Donald J. Trump, Declaring a National Emergency Concerning the Novel Coronavirus Disease (COVID-19) Outbreak, 
Proclamation 9994 of March 13, 2020, Federal Register , Vol. 85, No. 53, March 18, 2020.
3 Time and Date, “First Day of Stay at Home Order in the United States,” webpage, undated.
4 For our analysis, we used population weights and adjusted for sampling error in each ALP survey response question to 
allow for valid inferences about whether the results were representative of the broader population at large. Table A.2 in Appen-
dix A provides the weighted and unweighted summary statistics for the sample. We generated the weighted mean via “raking,” 
by which we match weighted marginal totals with the corresponding total in the population. In our case, this was performed 
using the Current Population Survey as a benchmark. In general, the unweighted sample tended to be older and more female 
than the population; the weights adjust for these differences. In the following sections, unless otherwise specified, we dis -
play only the weighted results, although we do not find that the weights make a significant difference in our results. For our 
weighting method, see Craig A. Bond and Howard J. Shatz, U.S. Consumer Preferences for Telephone and Internet Services , 
Santa Monica, Calif.: RAND Corporation, RR-1382-FCC, 2016, p. 8.

===== PDF_PAGE 41 =====
Individual-Level Analysis: Survey of U.S. Public Views of China 
27
Views of China as Threat or Opportunity (Question 1)
A plurality of individuals (41 percent of respondents) viewed China as a security threat and also an eco -
nomic opportunity (option f) out of a total of seven different ways to view China (see Table 4.1). Aggregat -
ing the responses for options a, b, and f, we find that the majority of surveyed U.S. respondents (75 percent) 
viewed China as, at least, a security threat. However, support for an overall hawkish line toward China was 
tempered by the fact that 49 percent of all respondents viewed China as an economic opportunity (aggregat -
ing responses to options d and f), compared with the 36 percent that viewed it as an economic threat to the 
United States (aggregating responses to options b and c). All of these responses are statistically significant, 
meaning they are not the outcome of chance and they represent the views of the broader population. This 
suggests that U.S. policymakers should be aware that a large portion of the U.S. population still wants to work 
with China economically despite viewing China as a security threat.
To understand what drives these results, we aggregated the survey responses into three different views 
of China and analyzed them in relation to our industry variable and demographic variables. The aggregated 
views of China, or outcome variables, are as follows:
1.
 se
curity threat = 1 if China is a security threat (i.e., options a, b, and f) and 0 otherwise
2. ec
onomic threat = 1 if China is an economic threat (i.e., options b and c) and 0 otherwise
3. ec
onomic opportunity = 1 if China is an economic opportunity (i.e., options d and f) and 0 otherwise
We find that these views are likely associated with a combination of demographic variables (Table A.3 in 
Appendix A provides the full results). We highlight here some of the strongest and most statistically signifi -
cant associations—that is, those at least statistically significant at the 5 percent level, unless otherwise stated; 
significant at the 5 percent level means that there is only a 5 percent or lower chance that they do not accu-
rately represent the views of the U.S. population:

• Older respondents are more likely to support strategic competition with China, as one year of age 
i
ncreases the perception of China as a security and economic threat by 0.44 percent and 0.33 percent 
per year, respectively, all else equal. Age also decreases viewing China as an economic opportunity by 
0.2 percent per year of age, all else equal. 
• Th
ose with no high school education are more likely to view China as a security threat. This same 
group, however, views China as less of an economic threat and more of an economic opportunity. 
Beyond this group, there was no clear association between education level and views of China as 
economic threat, although respondents with bachelor’s degrees view China more as an economic 
opportunity.
•

men are more likely to view China as less of a security threat and less of an economic threat. 
• Re
spondents in the Middle Atlantic, East North Central, and West North Central regions are least likely 
to view China as a security threat. 5 Living in these regions drops viewing China as a security threat by 
roughly 20 percent compared with the population as a whole. The Pacific region is similar, but lesser in 
magnitude, with a 13 percent decrease in view of China as a security threat.

• Re
publicans are likely to view China as a security threat (increase by 9 percent) and economic threat 
(increase by 19 percent). Viewing China as an economic opportunity also drops by 13.6 percent for 
Republicans. 
•
 Fi
nally, the results showed two interesting findings that are not statistically significant at the 5 percent 
level, but statistically significant at the 10 percent level: participants who self-identify as Asian or Pacific 
Islander are less likely (by 15.7 percent) to view China as a security threat, and participants born outside 
the United States are less likely (by 8.5 percent) to view China as a security threat.
We do not find any statistically significant relationship between industry and any of the three potential 
views of China. This suggests that there are not strong uniform views about China within each of the three 
U.S. business sectors.
We next analyze how respondents view three different U.S. economic policy options or actions through 
their answers to the remaining survey questions. 
Support for Limiting U.S. Goods and Services to Problematic PRC Actors 
(Question 2)
Nearly 60 percent of respondents said they would support U.S. companies not selling goods and services to 
Chinese actors that undermine U.S. interests or values (see Table 4.2). This result is statistically significant.
These results are likely associated with age (support for limiting services and goods increases by 0.2 per -
cent per year of age), and gender (support for limiting goods and services drops by 16 percent if the respon -
dent is female). 
We again do not find, using this method, any statistically significant relationship between industry and 
willingness to limit goods and services to PRC actors. Table A.4 in Appendix A provides the full results. 
5 These regions are based on U.S. Census data divisions. The Middle Atlantic comprises New Jersey, New York, and Pennsyl -
vania; the East North Central region comprises Indiana, Illinois, Michigan, Ohio, and Wisconsin; and the West North Central 
region comprises Iowa, Kansas, Minnesota, Missouri, Nebraska, North Dakota, and South Dakota.
6 The Pacific region comprises Alaska, California, Hawaii, Oregon, and Washington.

Individual-Level Analysis: Survey of U.S. Public Views of China 
29
Support for Limiting Chinese Nationals Studying or Working in the United States 
(Question 3)
In contrast to the support for limiting goods and services, a majority of respondents (64 percent) do not sup-
port limiting Chinese nationals from studying or working in the United States (see Table 4.3). 
As with other questions, the respondents’ backgrounds are linked to their answer to this question (see 
Table A.4 in Appendix A for full results):
• Th
ose living in urban areas may be more wary of competition from Chinese students or workers and are 
more inclined (by 6 percent) to support such limits. 
• Mo
re educated workers—those holding a bachelor’s degree or an advanced degree—are less likely to 
support limits on Chinese students by about 8 percent. However, those with no high school education 
are 17.4 percent more likely to support this option. 
• Th
ose who are not White / Caucasian, Black / African American, or Asian or Pacific Islander are also 
more likely to support such limits.
• Re
publicans are more willing to support limits; an affiliation with the Republican Party increases sup -
port for this policy by 31 percent.

For this question, we do find some variation by industry. Those working in the financial sector were 
7.4 percent less likely to support limiting PRC nationals, although this result is only statistically significant at 
the 10 percent level. This suggests that the finance industry may have a less negative (or more positive) view 
of Chinese nationals than do other industries. One explanation may be that the financial sector may depend 
more on, employ more, or interact more with Chinese nationals. 
Willingness to Buy a Mobile Phone from a PRC Company with Potential 
Military Links (Question 4)
Possibly reflecting a combination of privacy and security concerns, 83 percent of respondents did not want to 
purchase a Chinese mobile phone given knowledge that the Chinese military could use the phone to collect 
user information and intelligence (Table 4.4). 
Although the vast majority of respondents would not buy a Chinese phone, we do see two factors asso -
ciated with willingness to buy one (see Table A.4 in Appendix A): age (inclination to buy a Chinese phone 
decreases by 0.4 percent per year of age) and residing in the West North Central region (those in this region 
are 18 percent less likely to buy the phone).
7
We do not find any statistically significant relationship between employment industry and willingness to 
buy a Chinese mobile phone.
Summary
The U.S. public generally views China as a military or security threat, although this is tempered by a plu -
rality of respondents who view China as providing economic opportunity. Our survey results for the three 
economic actions or options that the United States or U.S. public could take suggest further nuance about the 
U.S. public’s views of China economically. On one hand, the U.S. public is generally supportive of restricting 
U.S. corporate products or services to China and boycotting problematic PRC products, suggesting a willing-
ness to curb potential economic gains from China to protect U.S. interests and values. At the same time, there 
is much less support for limiting Chinese workers and students in the United States. 
7 The West North Central region comprises Iowa, Kansas, Minnesota, Missouri, Nebraska, North Dakota, and South Dakota.

Individual-Level Analysis: Survey of U.S. Public Views of China 
31
In terms of potential drivers of respondents’ views or positions, we find that older people and Republicans 
tend to support taking a more competitive approach toward China and are more wary of China and Chinese 
products. Other demographic variables, such as higher education, gender (female), and whether respondents 
are born abroad, have varying degrees of moderating impact to discourage participants from supporting 
tougher economic actions against China or boycotting PRC products.
Among industries, we find limited evidence to suggest that those in the financial sector were less likely to 
support limits on PRC nationals studying or working in the United States. We did not find any other statisti-
cally significant results based on industry. It is also possible that our results are due to the limitations of our 
methodology for identifying participants by industry.
8
8 As discussed in the main text, the ALP survey employment category is coarse and does not ask for industry, but rather asks 
for occupation function. It is possible, for example, that both technology workers and financial workers fall under the category 
of “management occupations,” which we designated as part of only the financial industry.
