CHAPTER FIVE
Firm- and Sector-Level Analysis: 
Survey of Corporate Earnings Calls
U.S. policies that affect economic relations with China present a challenge to companies that buy Chinese 
products, sell to Chinese businesses and people, or invest in China. Leaders of these companies must assess 
the effects of these policies and inform company ownership of these effects and how the company might 
adjust. One way to capture these business leader views is through public statements that are, by regulation, 
intended to not be misleading—specifically, presentations in corporate earnings calls to company owners 
and business analysts. Accordingly, we conducted large-scale textual analysis of quarterly corporate earnings 
call transcripts from U.S. companies to analyze how different firms view China or its China-related opera -
tions. Most U.S. listed firms hold regular earnings conference calls with their shareholders (owners), analysts, 
and other interested parties during which management provides views on the firm’s past and future perfor -
mance and responds to question from call participants. Earnings call transcripts provide a valuable resource 
to track changes in corporate perspectives over time. 
Method
We analyzed quarterly earnings calls from two pools of companies. We first examined earnings calls from 
the 283 S&P 500 firms within our three sectors.
1 We were able to obtain earnings calls for the majority (252 
out of 283) of these firms, including for 128 manufacturing firms, 70 technology firms, and 54 financial 
firms. Appendix B lists the S&P companies we found earnings calls for, along with our categorization of 
these firms. 
We then supplemented and conducted an additional, separate analysis of firms in the Russell 2000 Index. 
While the S&P 500 contains the largest companies, the Russell 2000 contains much smaller companies. We 
first identified 350 firms with the lowest market capitalization to analyze: 150 manufacturing firms, 100 
technology firms, and 100 finance firms. However, we were able to find publicly available earnings calls only 
for small portion of these firms and had difficulty finding earnings calls particularly for the finance firms. 
As a result, we expanded our search for the earnings calls of the next 200 smallest finance firms. Across the 
total of 550 firms we searched for from the Russell 2000, we were able to obtain earnings calls for 193 firms 
(78 manufacturing firms, 63 technology firms, and 52 financial firms). Appendix C lists the Russell 2000 
companies along with our categorization of these firms. 
To track changes over time, we analyzed all quarterly earnings calls starting from Q1 2017 to Q2 2020. 
We used FD (Fair Disclosure) Wire, accessed via Nexis Uni, to retrieve earnings call transcripts using que -
ries generated from the firm names on our list. Not all firms had earnings calls available for all 14 quarters 
1 Based on our definition of the sectors, the full set of relevant S&P 500 firms includes 134 manufacturing firms, 83 technol -
ogy firms, and 66 financial firms.

===== PDF_PAGE 48 =====
Bridging the Gap
34
we covered.2 Nevertheless, the LexisNexis search results did produce enough data for the purpose of our 
analysis. 
On the S&P side, we were able to match at least one transcript from LexisNexis for 252 firms, and 83 firms 
had results for all 14 quarters. Of the Russell 2000 firms we searched for, we were only able to match 193 to 
results from LexisNexis. This is not surprising given the smaller size of these companies, and many (41 com-
panies) only had one quarter of data available. Figure 5.1 shows the distribution of quarterly calls retrieved by 
firm for the S&P 500 sample, and Figure 5.2 shows the same for the Russell 2000 sample.
Once the text from each earnings call was retrieved, we filtered for sentences in the transcripts that 
used keywords corresponding to the following terms: China , geopolitical, tariff, and trade war.
3 As depicted 
in Tables 5.1 and 5.2, only a portion of the earnings calls (and a subset of the companies within all data) 
mentioned these keywords; out of 2,208,971 total sentences in the S&P sample there were 26,276 (1.2 per -
cent) that matched our search criteria, and out of the 1,306,655 in the Russell 2000 sample there were 7,079 
(0.5 percent).
Given the amount of information available for S&P 500 firms, we divide and present analysis for S&P 500 
firms by sector and subsector. In contrast, because there are many fewer Russell 2000 firms that have publicly 
available earnings calls and mention China in these calls, we divide Russell 2000 firms only by sector. 
Using FinBERT, a pretrained natural language–processing model, to conduct sentiment analysis of finan-
cial texts, we then coded each sentence for whether the sentence conveyed a more positive, neutral, or nega -
tive sentiment.
4 FinBERT was chosen based on its relatively high performance for related tasks and low bar -
2 In some cases, the gaps were introduced by subtle variations in firm name that precluded a match on LexisNexis, and we 
did several passes to correct the most obvious mismatches. 
3 We discarded search results for another topic, Phase One , as it did not produce results related to our topic of study. The 
search terms we used were China (Beijing, China, Chinese, Shanghai); geopolitical (geopolitical, geo-political , geopolitics); tariff 
(tariff, tariffs); and trade war (trade tension, trade war ).
4 See Dogu Araci, Theofilos Papapanagiotou, and Paul v.d. B., “FinBERT: Financial Sentiment Analysis with BERT,” GitHub, 
undated; and Dogu Tan Araci, FinBERT: Financial Sentiment Analysis with Pre-Trained Language Models , thesis, University 
of Amsterdam, June 25, 2019.

riers to use.5 The combination of context provided by a search term hit in a sentence, and the sentiment score 
of that sentence is an indicator, albeit imperfect, for whether firms feel relatively optimistic or pessimistic 
about the search term topic (e.g., their business prospects, including operations, sales, or investments related 
to China). A more positive sentiment score in a sentence with the term China in it suggests that a firm is rela-
tively optimistic about its transactions or opportunities related to China, whereas a more negative sentiment 
reflects concern or pessimism with regard to the company’s China-related operations. 
Table 5.3 shows example sentences from our four subtopics that are representative of positive and negative 
scores. Sentiment scores have an upper bound of 1 (very positive) and a lower bound of –1 (very negative). A 
sentiment score of 0 indicates a relatively neutral view.

To estimate overall sentiment within each subsector or sector per quarter, we first averaged the sentence 
sentiment scores associated with each individual company to arrive at one sentiment value per company 
per quarter. We then averaged the sentiments across firms within the subsector or sector. 6 Tables 5.4 and 
5.5 depict the count, median, and standard deviation of average sentiment scores per firm per quarter. The 
count is the total number of firm-quarter observations, with each firm-quarter observation constituting the 
average sentiment associated with that firm in that quarter. The average is the average of all firm-quarter 
average sentiment scores, and the standard deviation is the standard deviation of all firm-quarter average 
sentiment scores. While the mean values are all positive (with the exception of a small set of regional banks), 
the standard deviations provide an indication of how the fluctuations veer into more negative territory. These 
standard deviations do not show a strong trend over the three-and-a-half-year period, either consistently 
narrowing or widening, although S&P manufacturing and three of its subsectors (excluding consumer sta -
ples) exhibit a modest trend. Average standard deviations for S&P manufacturing and three of its subsectors 
narrow through late 2018 and early 2019, and then rise through the Q2 2020, with quarterly volatility. Sub -
sequent analysis in the findings section examines the average sentiment scores by quarter to reveal trends 
over time. Appendix D provides details on the number of sentences and firms per quarter included in the 
earnings call analysis (see Tables D.1–D.2). It also provides graphs of the time trend of standard deviations 
(see Figures D.1–D.4).
6 Making each firm equal in the aggregation was done to guard against the possibility that our results would be skewed in 
case one or a few firms in a subsector or sector include significant discussion on China-related issues in their earnings calls. 
The flip side is that a single reference from one firm is given the same weight as lengthy discussion from another.

Relying on FinBERT’s natural language–processing model to conduct sentiment analysis has the benefit 
of lower human error in coding, but the automated system could create errors as well. Three types of errors 
present themselves. First, the sentiment could be incorrectly coded: a negative sentiment could be coded as 
positive and vice versa. Second, a sentiment could be off-topic despite the keyword; as a hypothetical exam -
ple, a statement such as “My birthday cake was delicious and served on fine china” could be rated as a positive 
sentiment related to China. Third, the strength of the sentiment could be incorrectly rated. 
We had two researchers independently check the ratings for the first two errors because whether the 
coding was right or wrong would be more obvious for these two errors, whereas more judgment would be 
needed—and larger differences of opinion could emerge—regarding the third error. Each researcher checked 
10 percent of the S&P 500 sample and 10 percent of the Russell 2000 sample, with the sentences to be checked 
randomly drawn. In addition, the sentences checked were not the same random sample for each researcher. 
The first researcher found that for both the S&P 500 and the Russell 2000 samples, more than 90 percent 
of the sentences in the random sample were correctly coded. The second researcher found more ambiguous

results. Sentences that were clearly positive or negative tended to have the correct numerical rating (more 
than 80 percent for each sample), but sentences with more neutral sentiments had more errors. If these errors 
are randomly distributed, our analysis should not be affected; however, if they occur systematically in one 
direction or the other, then our analysis could be in error. Full results of the validation exercise and a more 
detailed discussion appear in Appendix E.
Findings
The following sections first analyze the earnings call results for S&P 500 firms and then compare them 
with findings from Russell 2000 firms. Although large standard deviations make it difficult to assess results 
with confidence, the data suggest that manufacturing and technology firms were more concerned with (had 
decreasing sentiment toward) China-related operations from 2018 to late 2019. Although sentiment turned 
more positive for these two sectors in early 2020, the average sentiment scores did not return to early 2017 
levels. In contrast, S&P 500 financial firms saw a dip in sentiment in Q1 2018 (compared with late 2017) and 
a subsequent increase in sentiment from 2018 to Q2 2020.
7 Financial firms had a more positive sentiment 
score in Q2 2020 than Q1 2017. This suggests that financial firms could have viewed the actions taking by 
the Trump administration—or actions taken by China in response—as providing them with slightly more 
opportunities or benefits overall compared with costs. In contrast, manufacturing and technology firms may 
not have benefited as much and may have faced more difficulties and costs. We do not find any significant 
difference between how large (S&P 500) and small (Russell 2000) firms viewed China-related activities. 
S&P 500 Earnings Calls
Average sentiment associated with China remained positive for all three sectors from January 2017 to June 
2020. For the manufacturing and technology sectors, there is a general dip in sentiment from early 2017 until 
mid- to late 2019, with some upward movements afterward and particularly in early 2020 (see Figure 5.3). 
Even so, the data suggest growing concern, anxiety, or uncertainty with respect to China-exposed business 
operations and activities from late 2017 onward. Throughout this analysis, we focus on the sentiment point 
estimates. We note, however, that the large standard deviations in average sentiment of earnings calls make 
it difficult to assess these results—or the differences by sector—with confidence. 
Finance is the only sector in which S&P 500 firms felt more positive toward China by late 2019 / early 2020 
compared with early 2017. Even for financials, however, S&P 500 firms were less positive with respect to their 
China-related operations in late 2019 / early 2020 than during the Q4 2017. Much of the optimism and positive 
sentiment scores in Q4 2017 were driven by the increased access and new business opportunities in China.
8
As the United States embraced economic measures targeting China from early 2018 onward, such as the 
Section 301 tariffs, there were increasing numbers of S&P earnings calls referencing China (see Figure 5.4). 
Specifically, the number of China-related sentences in quarterly earnings calls by sector divided by total 
number of firms in the sector increased.

Technology Sector
Among the two subsectors of the technology sector, there appeared to be a similar general downward trend of 
sentiment from 2017 to late 2019. Software companies, in particular, saw a larger drop in sentiment regarding 
China-related business operations and activities from 2017 after a significant positive spike in sentiment in 
Q4 2017 (see Figure 5.6). This spike was chiefly a result of two things: earnings calls that referred to China’s 
online pay market, including the success and record number of sales from Alibaba’s Single’s Day on Novem-
ber 11, 2017, and the potential of the Chinese market;
10 and earnings calls that reported new opportunities 
or Chinese use of U.S. software. 11 Hardware companies mentioned China-related operations and activities 
more than software companies, and there was a significant increase in mentions of China in Q3 2018 and 
beyond (see Figure D.6).

Finance is the only sector in which earnings calls from all subsectors displayed at least one quarter where 
the average sentiment toward China-related business operations or activities was negative (see Figure 5.7).

with the Industrial and Commercial Bank of China.

Russell 2000 Earnings Calls
Analysis of earnings calls from Russell 2000 companies shows that smaller companies in the manufac -
turing and technology sectors generally shared the concerns of larger S&P 500 companies with regard to 
China-related business operations and activities. Similar to larger firms, sentiment toward China dropped 
for smaller manufacturing and technology firms from early 2017 until late 2019 before rebounding to some 
extent in early 2020. Smaller technology firms appeared to have a slightly more positive sentiment on China 
than larger technology firms. For these two Russell 2000 sectors, the average sentiment scores were based on 
at least 100 sentences per quarter. Similar to the S&P 500 data, large standard deviations in average sentiment 
scores make it difficult to assess results with confidence.
Earnings call data, however, suggest a slightly different picture for how smaller (Russell 2000) financial 
firms viewed China compared with larger (S&P 500) firms. Earnings calls from S&P 500 firms displayed a 
large spike in sentiment in Q4 2017, followed by a dip and then a generally positive upward trend from Q2 
2018 onward. There was more volatility in how smaller financial firms viewed China-related activities: the 
average sentiment score for Russell 2000 financial firms oscillated between positive and negative from Q1 
2017 to Q2 2018 (see Figure 5.8). Smaller financial firms had mainly positive average sentiment scores from 
Q3 2018 onward. Although this could suggest that smaller financial firms had a more pessimistic outlook 
on China prior to mid-2018, the Russell 2000 average sentiment scores were based on a very small sample of 
financial firms and China-related sentences (fewer than ten firms, and fewer than 20 sentences, from Q1 2017 
to Q1 2018) and we caution against giving too much weight to findings from only a handful of firms.
12 We 
also find that the average sentiment score of Russell 2000 financial firms is influenced by non-China-related 
sentences that used the term geopolitical .
13 The low score in Q1 2017, for example, is partially explained by 
concern over North Korea–related geopolitical tensions.14
Finally, similar to those of the S&P 500, smaller manufacturing and technology firms included more 
China-related sentences in their earnings calls than did finance companies. For Q1 2017 and Q4 2019 to Q2 
2020, the Russell 2000 data show that smaller technology companies had more China-related sentences in 
their earnings calls than did manufacturing firms (see Figure 5.9). 
Summary
The trade war and other U.S. policies that confronted China definitely caught the attention of the business 
community, with words related to those policies appearing more frequently in earnings calls throughout the 
period. For most sectors that we are analyzing, sentiment became less positive, but rarely negative. This sug -
gests that businesses were waiting to see how policies evolved and that they still viewed China as a legitimate 
and positive business partner, albeit one that might merit caution. Larger firms (those in the S&P 500) tended 
to have more positive sentiments toward the end of the period—particularly consumer staples manufactur -
ers, consumer discretionary manufacturers, asset managers and banks, and diversified finance companies.

CHAPTER SIX
Views from the Manufacturing Sector
Until it was surpassed by China in 2010, the United States had the world’s largest manufacturing sector as 
measured by output.1 The U.S. manufacturing sector contributed to 9.5 percent of U.S. real GDP in 2019 and 
8 percent of total nonfarm U.S. employment as of December 2019.2 It is large and diverse, with nearly 250,000 
firms, yet only 1.5 percent of the firms have more than 500 employees. 3 Companies in the sector include 
consumer discretionary manufacturers (e.g., General Motors, Nike), consumer staples (e.g., General Mills, 
Procter & Gamble), industrials (e.g., General Electric, Lockheed Martin), and materials (e.g., Dow, Nucorp). 
As noted above, we do not include firms that produce computer and electronic products in our definition of 
the manufacturing sector but instead consider them separately in the technology sector. The following sec -
tions analyze the U.S. manufacturing sector’s views of U.S.-China competition.
Background: Operations and Involvement in China
U.S. manufacturing companies have been active in China for decades since China’s opening up in 1978. 
Based on the 131 manufacturing firms in the S&P 500, the majority of the largest U.S. manufacturing com -
panies rely on China for 5 percent or less of their global sales (see Figure 6.1). Only 15 firms rely on China 
for more than 10 percent of their global sales. In addition to sales in China, approximately one-fourth of U.S. 
firms have production facilities in China.
4 Eighty-one percent depend to some extent on China for manufac-
tured parts and components, and 78 percent rely on China for materials. In contrast, only 30 percent of firms 
rely on China for research and design.
5 
The U.S. manufacturing sector leads U.S. FDI in China and is a top destination for PRC FDI in the 
United States. In 2019, U.S. manufacturers had $43.8 billion invested in China (38 percent of total U.S. FDI 
in China). Manufacturers of transportation equipment invested the most in China ($12.8 billion), followed 
by companies producing chemicals ($11.7 billion). Similarly, China had $5.6 billion invested in the U.S. 
manufacturing sector by 2019 (15 percent of all Chinese FDI in the United States). China invested the most 
1 Darrel M. West and Christian Lansang, Global Manufacturing Scorecard: How the U.S. Compares to 18 Other Nations , 
Washington, D.C.: Brookings Institution, July 10, 2018. 
2 For GDP share, see BEA, Gross Domestic Product by Industry: Fourth Quarter and Year 2019 , Washington, D.C.: DOC, 
April 6, 2020a. For employment share, see BLS, 2018. 
3 Three-fourths of manufacturing firms employ fewer than 20 employees. National Association of Manufacturers, “Facts 
About Manufacturing,” webpage, undated.
4 This figure is based on survey results of North American manufacturing companies. See Thomas Publishing Company, 
Thomas Industrial Survey: Coronavirus Impacts on North American Manufacturing , New York: Thomas Publishing Company, 
February 2020. 
5 Thomas, “New Survey: Coronavirus Impacts on North American Manufacturing, March 2020,” press release, New York, 
March 19, 2020.

Bridging the Gap
46
in other manufacturing ($2.5 billion), followed by transportation equipment ($1.6 billion) and machinery 
($591 million).6
Views of Competition from China and China-Related Operations
The growth of China’s economy and U.S.-China trade has significantly affected the sector. One study, for 
example, found that increased U.S. imports from China explain 16 percent of the decline of U.S. manufactur-
ing employment from 1990 through 2000, 26 percent of the decline from 2000 through 2007, and 21 percent 
over the entire period.
7 Another study found that although U.S. imports from China from 1995 to 2011 led 
to a loss of 2.0 million U.S. jobs (with 1.4 million in the manufacturing sector), the overall increase in U.S. 
exports to the world during this same period generated a gain of 3.7 million jobs (with 1.9 million in manu-
facturing) for the United States. In other words, despite imports from China, there was a net gain of 0.5 mil-
lion jobs in the U.S. manufacturing sector during this period.
8 Similarly, other studies show that U.S. exports 
to China create American jobs. One report found that in 2015, U.S. exports to China directly supported 
1.5 million jobs and indirectly supported another 0.3 million jobs.
9 However, as the U.S. manufacturing 
goods trade deficit with China grew from $75 billion in 2002 to $233 billion in 2017, there is growing concern 
that there will be more job losses in the manufacturing sector due to import competition from China.
10
6 BEA, “Balance of Payments and Direct Investment Position Data,” webpage, undated b.
7 Autor, Dorn, and Hanson, 2013.
8 Robert C. Feenstra and Akira Sasahara, “The ‘China Shock,’ Exports and U.S. Employment: A Global Input-Output 
Analysis,” Cambridge, Mass.: National Bureau of Economic Research, NBER Working Paper 24022, November 2017.
9 Oxford Economics, Understanding the U.S.-China Trade Relationship , Washington, D.C.: U.S.-China Business Council, 
January 2017. Because a good portion of U.S. exports to China include agricultural commodities, not all of these employment 
gains are in the manufacturing sector.

July 20, 2020. Robert E. Scott, The China Toll Deepens: Growth in the Bilateral Trade Deficit Between 2001 and 2017 Cost 3.4

Views from the Manufacturing Sector
47
Manufacturing companies view China as engaging in unfair, distortionary, and protectionist policies to 
facilitate market gains globally and in the United States. U.S. steel producers, for example, have criticized 
China for a range of measures: dumping steel below production cost; IP theft, including alleged hacking 
into U.S. Steel; massive PRC government subsidies of its steel industry, including state ownership of major -
ity shares in firms and shielding them from competing with private enterprises; manipulating its currency 
(weakening its value) to subsidize PRC exports;
11 and exploiting loopholes to export more steel, including not 
reporting steel exports in a timely manner to international organizations, transshipment of steel, and chang -
ing the labels of PRC steel products to read “Made in Vietnam.”12 
As a diverse sector, manufacturing shares many of the concerns identified by the U.S. business commu -
nity with respect to operating in China. According to the U.S. Chamber of Commerce’s 2017 survey, the top 
ten challenges were (1) competition with Chinese companies in China; (2) difficulties obtaining licenses and 
approvals; (3) barriers to investing in China; (4) uneven enforcement of PRC regulations and laws or uncer -
tain PRC policy climate; (5) lack of IP rights enforcement or protection; (6) cybersecurity and technology 
security, including the effects of China’s cyberregulation and data regulation on business operations; (7) cost 
increases in China; (8) the effects of U.S.-China relations on business; (9) data flow barriers; and (10) restric-
tive PRC capital controls.
13 
As U.S.-China trade tensions grew from 2017 onward, U.S. companies suffered from higher tariffs levied 
by both countries and faced a more uncertain business environment in China. Chinese suppliers and cus -
tomers were wary of doing business with U.S. companies. There was increased PRC scrutiny on U.S. firms 
and evidence that Chinese regulators excluded American companies from bids or tenders or delayed U.S. 
requests for approval of licenses or products.
14
While all these policies caused challenges for U.S. manufacturing firms, their effects may have varied 
based on the extent to which the firm’s products require more R&D. A 2020 survey suggested that R&D-
intensive industries—which included aerospace companies—were most concerned with “uncertainties in the 
U.S.-China economic relationship and bilateral tariffs” than any other issue. These same companies also had 
more concerns about “uncertain Chinese policy environment” and “market access barriers or government 
policies that disadvantage foreign companies” (two factors that were their second and third concerns) than 
did resources and industrial firms, which viewed these two issues as the least concerning out of a range of 
eight issues. Instead, resources and industrial firms, which included auto companies and companies engaged 
in machinery equipment, and chemicals, were most concerned about slower growth in China than any other 
issue.
15 
Million U.S. Jobs, with Losses in Every State and Congressional District , Washington, D.C.: Economic Policy Institute, Octo-
ber 23, 2018. 
11 Despite criticism from the U.S. manufacturing sector, the United States only officially designated China a currency manip -
ulator from 1992 to 1994 and again from 2019 to 2020.

Major U.S. Policy Actions or PRC Responses That Affected the Sector
Since 2017, almost all of the major U.S. economic actions against China and corresponding PRC responses 
have targeted the manufacturing sector. Table 6.1 displays the three major tariff actions that the United States 
took and PRC retaliation. Tariffs levied under Section 201 of the 1974 Trade Act (on solar cells and residen -
tial washers) and Section 232 of the 1962 Trade Expansion Act (on steel and aluminum imports) were global 
tariffs that also applied to China. In contrast, Section 301 tariffs were initiated in response to China’s unfair 
acts, policies, and practices related to technology transfer, IP, and innovation.
17 All three tariff actions cov -
ered manufacturing inputs or goods, with the Section 232 tariffs on imported aluminum and steel shielding 
U.S. producers from competition but also raising prices for or constraining supply to a wide range of U.S. 
industries dependent on aluminum and steel inputs. China responded by imposing several rounds of tariff 
increases on the United States in 2018 and 2019, raising these import taxes on a combined total of approxi -
mately $185 billion worth of U.S. manufactured and other goods across two years. 
In addition to imposing tariffs on PRC products, increased U.S. scrutiny on foreign (PRC) investment in 
the United States has also introduced more friction on completing international investment in the manu -
facturing sector. In 2018 the United States enacted FIRRMA, which expanded the power of the Commit -
tee on Foreign Investment in the United States (CFIUS) to review foreign transactions or investments for 
national security purposes.
18 Under FIRRMA, and in 2018 via a pilot program, CFIUS requirements initially 
expanded mandatory filing requirements to cover transactions involving controlling investments by foreign 
persons in U.S. businesses. The new requirements targeted any transactions to produce, design, test, manu -
facture, fabricate, or develop critical technologies in one or more of 27 key industries. Twenty-three out of 27 
industries in the pilot program have NAICS codes that correspond to the manufacturing sector as defined 
16 Brian Wiborg, “Re: Ace Hardware Corporation: Comments in Opposition to Inclusion of Home Improvement Articles in 
USTR Section 301 Annex Docket No. USTR-2019-0004,” correspondence from Ace Hardware Corporation to Robert Light -
hizer, June 17, 2019. 
17 China is not among the top ten countries from which the United States imports steel directly. In terms of quantity, China 
accounted for approximately 2 percent of U.S. steel imports in 2017; in terms of dollar value, it made up 3 percent of steel 
imports in 2017. China also does not export aluminum in commodity form to the United States but ships semifinished alu -
minum products. China, however, is a major producer of both steel and aluminum: it is the world’s largest steel producer, and 
it accounted for more than half of the world’s primary aluminum production in 2019. Its massive production of both mate -
rials affects international capacity and prices. See Rachel F. Fefer, Keigh E. Hammond, Vivian C. Jones, Brandon J. Murrill, 
Michaela D. Platzer, and Brock R. Williams, Section 232 Investigations: Overview and Issues for Congress , Washington, D.C.: 
Congressional Research Service, R45249, August 24, 2020; U.S. International Trade Administration, “Steel Imports Report: 
United States,” Global Steel Monitor , May 2020b; and John Kruzel, “How Much Steel Does the U.S. Import from China?” 
Politifact, March 8, 2019. 
18 U.S. Department of the Treasury, Summary of the Foreign Investment Risk Review Modernization Act of 2018 , Washington, 
D.C.: U.S. Department of the Treasury, undated a.

Responses to Increased U.S.-China Competition
This section explores how the U.S. manufacturing sector is responding to the aforementioned U.S. and PRC 
economic actions. This involves considering at least four ways tariffs could change industry employment, 
wages, sales, and other characteristics: (1) tariffs protect some U.S. manufacturers from import competition, 
(2) tariffs increase the cost of imported PRC intermediate inputs and hurt some manufacturers producing for 
export and the U.S. domestic market, (3) PRC retaliatory actions disadvantage U.S. firms exporting to China, 
and (4) tariffs create uncertainty that could discourage significant investment or other operations. We com -
bine these considerations with the additional consequence of limiting PRC investment in the manufacturing 
sector and export restrictions on U.S. (higher-value) manufacture sales to China. 
20 These include “(a) A license or other approval issued by the Department of State under the ITAR [International Traffic in 
Arms Regulations]; (b) A license from the DOC under the EAR; (c) A specific or general authorization from the Department 
of Energy under the regulations governing assistance to foreign atomic energy activities at 10 CFR part 810 other than the 
general authorization described in 10 CFR 810.6(a); or (d) A specific license from the Nuclear Regulatory Commission under 
the regulations governing the export or import of nuclear equipment and material at 10 CFR part 110.” U.S. Department of 
the Treasury, Office of Investment Security, “Provisions Pertaining to Certain Investments in the United States by Foreign 
Persons,” Federal Register, Vol. 85, No. 179, September 15, 2020b. 
21 The 14 technologies are AI and machine learning technology; quantum information and sensing technology; additive 
manufacturing (3D printing); advanced computing technology; position, navigation, and timing technology; data analytics 
technology; brain-computer interfaces; advanced materials; biotechnology; microprocessor technology; logistics technology; 
robotics; advanced surveillance technologies; and hypersonics. Barkin, 2020.

Major Adjustments or Changes in Business Activities
Here we look at changes in U.S. manufacturing activities across five main business functions: operations, 
sales and marketing, finance, R&D, and employment and human resources, as well as changes since the onset 
of COVID-19. 
Operations
U.S. imports of manufactured goods from China declined from $540 billion in 2018 to $452 billion in 2019, 
and U.S. goods exports to China—and globally—declined.
26 It is difficult to assess how many of those declines 
were attributable directly to trade actions. However, the decrease in imports from China was accompanied by 
an increase in imports ($31 billion) from Asian low-cost countries other than China and increase in imports 
($13 billion) from Mexico.
27 These statistics suggest that while U.S. policies may have reduced imports from 
China, it is not clear that U.S. manufacturing sector performance improved, particularly as regard its exports.
U.S. manufacturing firms operating in China are considering a range of responses to the tariffs and increas-
ing U.S.-China trade tensions, including moving manufacturing and supply chains out of both China and the 
United States. According to the American Chamber of Commerce’s survey of China members in 2019, 6 per-
cent of technology and other R&D-intensive industries are considering relocating some or all of manufactur-
ing out of China. Similarly, 7 percent of these firms are considering relocating some or all manufacturing out 
of the United States. Eighteen percent of firms intend to adjust their supply chains by sourcing components 
and assembly outside China, and 20 percent seek to source components and assembly outside the United 
States. For resources and industrial firms, 15 percent of firms seek to relocate some or all manufacturing out of 
China, and 5 percent seek to relocate some or all manufacturing out of the United States.
28 Thirty-two percent 
of these firms are adjusting supply chains to countries other than China, and the same percentage of firms are 
also adjusting their supply chains to countries other than the United States.
29 These figures, backed by exam-
ples provided by manufacturing groups, suggest that U.S. policies and PRC retaliation have complicated and 
increased the costs of operations for U.S. firms with activities in China and the United States.
30
Compared to U.S. firms in other sectors, U.S. manufacturing firms are most concerned with relocating 
manufacturing and adjusting supply chains. This does not mean, however, that U.S. manufacturers are keen 
on exiting China. Survey results show the opposite: U.S. manufacturing firms are the least likely to exit China 
(only 1 percent to 2 percent have considered exiting China) compared with other types of U.S. firms (2 per -
cent to 5 percent).
31 
26 U.S. goods imports from China had increased between 2017 and 2018 before dropping in 2019. U.S. goods exports to China 
declined from $132 billion in 2017 to $108 billion in 2019. Global U.S. goods exports increased from 2017 ($1,557 billion), but 
decreased from $1,666 billion in 2018 to $1,643 billion in 2019. The U.S. manufactured goods trade deficit with China also 
declined. See BLS, “U.S. International Trade in Goods and Services, June 2020,” webpage, August 5, 2020b.
27 Vietnam accounted for 46 percent of the increase in U.S. imports from other Asian countries. Some of this increase is likely 
caused by PRC transshipment to Vietnam to dodge U.S. tariffs. See Van den Bossche, Levering, et al., undated b. 
28 Resources and industrial firms include those engaged in agribusiness; automotive and transportation vehicle manufactur -
ing; machinery, equipment, systems, and controls manufacturing; oil, gas, and energy; and other industrial endeavors, such as 
chemical manufacturing, mining, paper production, and packaging. American Chamber of Commerce in China, 2020, p. 16.
29 American Chamber of Commerce in China, 2020, p. 79.
30 Linda Dempsey, “Re: USTR-2019-0004; Request for Comments Concerning Proposed Modification of Action Pursuant to

correspondence from National Association of Manufacturers to Robert Lighthizer, June 12, 2019, p. 6; American Chemistry

31 American Chamber of Commerce in China, 2020, p. 79.

===== PDF_PAGE 66 =====
Bridging the Gap
52
This reluctance to exit China is likely driven by a number of factors. First, China has a positive manufac-
turing environment with low cost; skilled labor; and relatively lax labor, health and safety, and environmen -
tal regulations. As one business executive noted, many manufacturing production processes have a layer of 
activities in China to take advantage of these conditions, and this is particularly the case for operations that 
are capital-intensive or less environmentally friendly (e.g., lithium-ion battery production).
32 
Second, China is a large market with growth opportunities and a number of U.S. companies are located 
in China to serve Chinese customers. The working population in China is larger than that of Europe and the 
United States combined, and as incomes in China rise, the Chinese consumer market is one in which U.S. com-
panies will want to have a stake.
33 The size of the Chinese market has caused some to believe that China could 
be a “self-sustaining growth story” and that companies could survive by selling only to the Chinese market.34
Third, U.S. companies face difficulty (in both time and cost) finding new suppliers from other countries. 
According to the Association of Home Appliance Manufacturers, for example, manufacturers may require 
up to three years to qualify new suppliers from other countries due to the need to consider a range of factors 
including “tooling, sourcing of raw materials, testing, capacity, quality, regulatory compliance, social compli-
ance, safety approvals, engineering time and investment, existing inventory at the current supplier, and lead 
time.” Shifting sourcing countries for widely used home appliance parts primarily only produced in China—
such as toasters, coffeemakers, and teamakers—“would take over five years resulting in product shortages 
and/or noticeable price increases.”
35
Sales and Marketing
Across the sector, there are reports that U.S. and PRC actions have caused firms to increase the price of 
their products, which has resulted in lost sales and market shares. Many manufacturers report that tariffs 
levied on imported products are from wholly owned U.S. operations, in which there is no Chinese partner 
or unfair PRC practice involved that would result in technology transfer, increasing the costs to U.S. firms.
36 
Many companies have had no choice but to pass the costs on to customers. Section 201 tariffs on solar cells 
and residential washers, for example, caused manufacturers to increase the cost of U.S. washing machines by 
12 percent after the tariff was implemented.
37 
The roller-coaster U.S. steel producers experienced demonstrates an interesting case of multiple U.S. and 
PRC actions contributing to a short-term boost (an increase in sales and profits) complicated by longer-term 
declines in prices and production. In the immediate months after the passing of Section 232 tariffs, U.S. steel 
producers increased steel prices over 20 percent, generating significant criticism from and costs for down -
stream U.S. manufacturing companies.
38 Protectionism and increased profits encouraged U.S. steel produc -
ers to boost domestic capacity, including restarting furnaces. These activities occurred as the global economy 
cooled (due to a range of factors), the United States imposed Section 301 tariffs and China retaliated, and

33 Goldman Sachs, “The Rise of China’s New Consumer Class,” webpage, undated b.

Views from the Manufacturing Sector
53
demand for steel products fell. By mid- to late 2019, U.S. steel prices fell, and some leading U.S. steel compa-
nies began losing significant market value and had to idle production capacity. 39
In contrast to steel producers, the U.S. automotive industry likely suffered the most harm from U.S. and 
PRC actions. Auto producers faced higher input costs for steel, U.S. tariffs on $20 billion worth of auto parts 
imported from China annually, and retaliatory PRC tariffs on $13 billion worth of assembled motor vehi -
cle exports to China annually. 40 The auto industry was also threatened with the potential that the United 
States could impose additional Section 232 tariffs on automobiles and auto parts (which appears to have been 
shelved by the Trump administration). Modeling various potential future scenarios, the Center for Automo -
tive Research found sizable declines in annual auto sales, price increases, decreases in total car dealership 
revenue, and declines in total U.S. employment.
41
With respect to operations in China in 2018, a sizable portion of U.S. firms had to increase the sale price 
of their products (30 percent) and experienced a decrease in demand for products (29 percent), increased 
costs of manufactured products (33 percent), loss of business to competitors (21 percent), and reduced profits 
(39 percent) and revenues (26 percent). Surveys suggest that more U.S. firms experienced these negative busi-
ness effects in 2018 than in 2019.
42 
This does not mean, however, that all U.S. manufacturing firms suffered sales and market losses during 
this period. Some firms did not experience as great a degree of negative business consequences because the 
goods they produce or the immediate products required for production of their goods were not targeted by 
the tariffs that the United States or China imposed on each other. Some of these firms saw increases in sales 
and market shares as they took advantage of the growing Chinese consumer market. Athletic goods manu -
facturer Nike, for example, was largely unaffected by U.S.-China trade tensions and saw large sales gains in 
China in 2018 and 2019.
43 
Finance 
Compared to a high in 2016, overall Chinese investment in the U.S. manufacturing sector was lower annually 
from 2017 to 2019. Figure 6.2 shows PRC investment in five different subsectors. Across all subsectors, PRC 
investment dipped in 2017 before increasing in 2018 and 2019, with growth concentrated in two subsectors: 
consumer products and services and machinery.
A number of factors explain the fluctuation and relatively lower PRC investment in recent years.
44 Among 
these, it is possible that heightened U.S. security concerns, increased U.S. scrutiny under CFIUS, and bilateral

Employment and Human Resources 
Employment grew significantly from January 2017 to February 2019, when the sector began to see some 
months with job losses and other months with job increases before a drop in employment from February 
2020 onward due to COVID-19.
54 It is likely that the growth in manufacturing employment from 2017 to 
early 2019 was due to the positive effects of U.S. corporate tax cuts, as well as regulatory reform, 55 and since 
the Section 301 tariffs were not implemented until mid-2018, it may have taken months for U.S. and PRC 
trade actions to have an effect on employment.
56
There is abundant anecdotal evidence across the sector that suggests that the tariffs—particularly the 
more extensive Section 301 tariffs—have caused firms to delay or reduce hiring in the United States (particu-
larly for those firms shifting production out of the country) and scale back on wage increases or other bene-
fits for U.S. employees. Economists estimate that, looking at the sector as a whole, the small positive effect on 
U.S. employment from tariff import protection on U.S. producers is more than offset by the larger negative

effects of rising input costs and retaliatory tariffs. 57 According to the National Association of Manufacturers, 
“for many companies, these types of cost-cutting efforts negate other benefits they may have expected from 
administration-led policy changes such as domestic tax reform.” 58 
There is some evidence that these trade policies may also have negatively affected U.S. manufacturing 
firms’ operations in China; a small proportion of U.S. firms (all types, not just manufacturing-specific firms) 
in China have reported reductions in staff in 2018 and 2019 as a result of the bilateral tariffs.
59 U.S. multina-
tional manufacturing enterprises employed approximately 527,100 employees in China in 2018, lower than 
the 562,700 employees employed in China in 2017.
60
Changes Since the Onset of COVID-19
For U.S. manufacturers, COVID-19 has exacerbated many of the costs discussed above, and it is likely that 
the pandemic swamped any effects of China-specific trade and investment policies. By April 2020, surveys 
of North American manufacturing firms showed that 41 percent of firms experienced a decrease in demand 
for their products; industries experienced at least a 38 percent drop in international demand for products or 
services; over half of all firms experienced shutdown of essential business, affecting more than 70 percent of 
firms in the transportation, chemical, engineering, automotive, and construction sectors; and 28 percent of 
firms had layoffs as a result of COVID-19, with another 16 percent expecting to lay off workers. More North 
American companies (64 percent of all manufacturers) were willing to move production back home in April 
2020 than in February or March 2020.
61 The pandemic also may have added incentives for some firms to shift 
some production out of China to build greater resilience in their supply chains, establishing a greater number 
of sources for inputs so as to decrease reliance on any one source—especially China.
62
Responses to Shape U.S. Policies or PRC Responses
While there has been relatively less public opposition to Section 201 tariffs, U.S. manufacturing companies 
and representatives have taken clear public positions for or against Section 232 and Section 301 tariffs. Not 
surprisingly, steel and aluminum producers have strongly supported and defended the need for Section 232 
steel and aluminum tariffs. Downstream manufacturing companies, including users of steel and aluminum 
products, have pointed out that the tariffs benefit a small number of producers at the cost of a larger number 
of manufacturing companies. Ford Motor Company executives, for example, criticized the U.S. government 
for its steel tariffs, arguing that the tariffs have made steel more expensive in the United States than in any 
other market.
63 
In contrast, there has been very little support for Section 301 tariffs. Most companies agreed with the 
Trump administration’s desire to combat unfair Chinese trade practices but did not believe that tariffs were 
the best or main way to do so. The National Association of Manufacturers, for example, urged the U.S. gov -

rity while not burdening or limiting friendly foreign investors. 67 Experts have pointed out that the new U.S. 
regulations and export restrictions introduce more compliance burdens on U.S. companies continuing to do 
business with China, and firms will need to allocate additional labor to ensure compliance and handle the 
necessary paperwork. It is also very likely that new requirements, including the need for U.S. government 
approval for licenses or reviews, could delay planned transactions by months, potentially scuttling proposed 
business deals.
68 It is possible that some businesses are adopting a wait-and-see mode to determine their

Overall Effect of Increased U.S.-China Competition on the Sector
While the U.S. manufacturing sector supported the Trump administration’s goal of countering unfair PRC 
economic practices, it has been generally wary of the specific approach and tools the administration embraced 
to do so. Most U.S. manufacturing companies with operations in China wanted to continue to manufacture 
products there or sell to the Chinese market. There was widespread concern within the sector that Section 
301 tariffs against China were doing more harm than good. 
In general, available evidence suggests that the measures that the Trump administration embraced to 
compete with China have had an overall negative effect—imposing more costs than benefits—on the U.S. 
manufacturing sector across the five main business functions: operations, sales and marketing, finance, 
R&D, and employment and human resources (see Table 6.3). 
This does not mean, however, that the U.S. manufacturing sector will not support increased U.S.-China 
economic competition. The sector is likely to support more tailored, specific policies targeting problematic 
PRC practices instead of broader-brush actions such as tariffs imposed on a wide range of PRC products. It is 
likely to support the use of more economic “sticks” against China if such measures are balanced by offering 
China incentives, or “carrots,” and if such actions are part of coordinated multilateral efforts (in concert with 
key U.S. allies and partners) to level the playing field against China. The unknown factor in such a policy 
direction is how well these policies will work overall and in comparison with current policy directions.

CHAPTER SEVEN
Views from the Technology Sector
America’s IT and communications technology production sector is often considered the most advanced in 
the world. This sector contributed to 4.9 percent of U.S. real GDP in 2019 and 1.8 percent of total farm U.S. 
employment.
1 Like the manufacturing sector, it is large and diverse, with only 25.8 percent of firms having 
more than 500 employees in 2017.2
Technology encompasses a wide variety of phenomena, from genomics to materials. For technology, we 
focus on two types of groups. The first group comprises software companies that provide internet services, 
software, and IT services. These companies may operate online databases or interactive services (e.g., search 
engines and social networking), provide technical assistance (e.g., data analytics and numeric processing), or 
otherwise design software for personal or business use. These companies tend not to require fixed presences 
in operating countries or manufacturing hubs. Examples of these companies include Facebook, Google, and 
Microsoft. The second group comprises hardware companies that manufacture or operate tangible equip -
ment such as telecommunications devices (e.g., routers, phones), semiconductors, and other physical compo-
nents (e.g., computers, peripherals, and memory storage). Examples of such companies include Apple, Intel, 
Nvidia, and Qualcomm. Of the 83 technology companies within the S&P 500, we classify 40 of them as soft -
ware firms and 43 as hardware firms. 
The following sections look at how the technology sector views U.S.-China competition.
Background: Operations and Involvement in China
The technology sector is involved in China through two primary means: (1) manufacturing their products 
and (2) selling their products, which need not be manufactured in China, to Chinese customers.
3 In the 
case of the former, U.S. companies have substantial involvement in producing their products in China. This 
can be measured using import activity. According to one analysis, the U.S. imports 93 percent of laptops, 
73 percent of mobile phones, 52 percent of projectors, 88 percent of video game consoles, 58 percent of micro-
phones, and 51 percent of lithium-ion batteries from China.
4 The Apple case is particularly well known. 
1 BEA, “GDP by Industry,” webpage, last modified January 5, 2022; the tech sector’s contribution to GDP was calculated by 
adding lines 19, 52, and 53; BLS, “Current Employment Statistics—CES (National),” Table 7, “Most Recent Industry-Specific 
Employment Peak and Trough, and Changes from Peak and Trough to Current Employment, Seasonally Adjusted (in Thou -
sands),” May 21, 2020a; the tech sector’s contribution was calculated by summing computer and electronic products; telecom -
munications; data processing, hosting and related services; and other information services.
2 Statista, “Breakdown of Information Technology (IT) Employment Figures in the United States in 2017, by Company Size,” 
webpage, January 2017.
3 It is worth noting that American companies do perform R&D in China. However, as described in later sections, these 
offices are of minimal importance.
4 James Rogers, Andrew Foxall, Matthew Henderson, and Sam Armstrong, Breaking the China Supply Chain: How the “Five 
Eyes” Can Decouple from Strategic Dependency , London: Henry Jackson Society, May 2020, p. 26.

===== PDF_PAGE 76 =====
Bridging the Gap
62
Aside from the hundreds of thousands of contractors that Apple indirectly hires (via Foxconn) to assemble 
iPhones,5 most of Apple’s immediate suppliers have manufacturing operations in China.6 Many of these sup-
pliers have their own Tier 2 suppliers for their products. Although the suppliers of these Tier 2 companies 
are difficult to measure, it likely only entangles Apple further into China. This supply chain is fragile. When 
Chinese factories shuttered to contain the COVID-19 outbreak, Apple ran into supply issues, running low 
on replacement parts for iPhones.
7 This situation is likely to complicate any firm’s exit from China, for firms 
cannot readily replace the availability of midlevel-skilled Chinese employees, seasonal workers, and infra-
structure that has been built up over decades.
The second primary means of involvement in China is selling to Chinese firms. Across the S&P 500 tech-
nology sector, median sales were $9.9 billion in 2019, of which 4.45 percent was to Chinese clients.
8 Some 
firms, however, rely on Chinese sales much more than their other technology counterparts. For the most part, 
software companies do not substantially rely on Chinese sales, while hardware companies are more dependent 
on them. For software firms in 2019, the median firm had $10.2 billion in sales, with 3.3 percent to China. The 
median hardware firm had $9.7 billion in sales in 2019, with 15.7 percent to China. The high exposure of the 
hardware industry to China is driven in large part by the semiconductor industry. The median semiconductor 
firm had $6.7 billion in sales in 2019, with 27 percent of those sales being made to China. Of the top 20 technol-
ogy firms with exposure to China, 15 operate in the semiconductor industry (see Figure 7.1).
Furthermore, as shown in Figure 7.1, engagement with China is skewed, as are overall sales. For example, 
among all technology firms, even though median sales were $9.9 billion, the average was $26.8 billion, with 
the maximum being $280.5 billion. And while the median of sales to China was 4.45 percent, the average 
was 10.7 percent, with the maximum being 55.0 percent. Figures are similarly skewed for total sales in both

the software and hardware sectors, although the percentage of sales to China for each is not. For the software 
sector, while the median percentage of sales to China was 3.3 percent, the mean was 4.2 percent; for the hard-
ware sector, those figures were 15.7 percent and 16.5 percent, respectively.
In general, the U.S. technology sector (including other non-S&P 500 companies) has made substantial 
investments in China. According to the BEA, in 2019, technology firms invested a total of $12.6 billion in 
China: firms specializing in “computer and electronic products” and “information” had $10.4 billion and 
$2.2 billion invested, respectively. In 2019, Chinese investment in the United States in “computer and elec -
tronic products” amounted to $572 million, and investment in “information” totaled $2.3 billion.
9
It is worth noting that looking at current sales only measures the present situation and omits any plans 
that firms may have to expand operations. Some companies, such as PayPal and Visa, have taken such actions 
as acquiring a local license to operate and acquiring local companies to expand operations in China. To the 
extent that companies may have substantial plans to expand operations, U.S.-China competition may create 
tensions between the U.S. government and companies.
Views of Competition with China and China-Related Operations
The technology sector has operated in China for decades. Hardware companies have sought to use the coun-
try as a low-cost manufacturing base. These ties have continued with China producing much of the United 
States’ imported electronics. Furthermore, as China has grown economically, U.S. companies—both hard -
ware and software—also have increasingly tried to access the domestic Chinese market for its large poten -
tial. The 83 S&P 500 companies that we classified as being in the technology sector collectively brought in 
$18.1 billion in revenue from China in 2019. The sector seeks to use China as a manufacturing hub while 
being able to sell its products to local consumers.
U.S. firms face a number of obstacles in their China-related operations. Some of these involve difficulties 
associated with operating in a foreign environment; for example, Amazon was unable to gain a foothold in 
the Chinese market, attributable to “cutthroat local competitors” and inability to “adapt well to the unique 
Chinese market.”
10 Other hurdles that U.S. companies mention include cyberespionage against U.S. firms, 
forced IP transfers, and protectionist policies that prevent U.S. firms from fully competing and favor domes-
tic Chinese companies.
11 There is also some concern that U.S. policymakers may impose actions to limit the 
ability of U.S. firms to sell or work with Chinese firms (e.g., export controls), especially if such action is unco-
ordinated with allied countries and results in the displacement of U.S. firms by firms in U.S.-allied countries 
in the Chinese market.
12
U.S. companies also have concerns about how Chinese firms operate and compete in the Chinese market 
or abroad. These concerns stem from an unlevel playing field caused by the protection and support the Chi -
nese government provides Chinese firms (e.g., government subsidies). Beijing, for example, provided sub -
stantial money to fund Huawei’s R&D. 13 U.S. companies are also wary of the willingness of some Chinese 
9 BEA, undated b. The BEA category “information” includes more subcategories than are included in our definition of the 
technology sector. Unfortunately, more granular figures were not available from the BEA.

firms to use problematic means (e.g., IP theft) to edge out competition. The U.S. Department of Justice, for 
example, indicted a state-owned Chinese company for IP theft from Micron Technologies, its competitor. 14 
According to an executive at a business group, these tactics, in combination with larger PRC geopolitical 
efforts such as the BRI, could further disadvantage U.S. companies as they move into China or third coun -
tries (including those in Europe), potentially costing revenue streams or threatening business solvency.

Major U.S. Policy Actions or PRC Responses That Affected the Sector
All the major U.S. policy actions and PRC responses that affected the manufacturing sector (discussed in 
Chapter Six) also affected the technology sector. These include the reciprocal tariff actions that culminated 
in the Phase One deal in January 2020 that required China to bolster IP measures, implement a plan to deal 
with counterfeit goods, improve the protection of trade secrets, and increase penalties for infringements.
16 
The Phase One deal, however, left broader issues unresolved, including reciprocal market access, subsidies for 
Chinese firms, and cross-border data flows.
17
Along with expanded export restrictions on goods produced with sensitive U.S. technologies and other 
materials with dual civil-military applications, 18 the DOC proposed a rule that would empower it to regu -
late certain transactions between firms in the United States and “foreign adversaries” in the name of secur -
ing the information and communications technology and services (ICTS) supply chain. 19 These actions 
have, for example, restricted the ability of Huawei to purchase semiconductors produced using U.S. technol-
ogy and has tightened exports to China’s largest chipmaker, Semiconductor Manufacturing International 
Corporation.
20 
The United States has also taken policy actions to counter what then–Federal Bureau of Investigation 
director Christopher Wray called a “comprehensive threat to America’s innovation [and] to our economic 
security.”

investigation and prosecution of trade secret theft and economic espionage, to better countering threats

has the same level of state backing, this has produced a situation in which few Western companies (and no U.S. companies) 
make critical 5G products. This Chinese support for Huawei, as Butcher summarizes, is aimed “to destroy competitors like 
Ericsson, Nokia and Samsung.” See “How China’s Huawei Took the Lead over U.S. Companies in 5G Technology,” Washing -

to fund a Western competitor to large Chinese technology corporations in critical areas such as 5G. 27 These 
represent a more aggressive position against Chinese actions than had occurred before 2017. 
On the Chinese side, Beijing considered retaliatory measures to U.S. actions, including tariffs on technol-
ogy sector imports from the United States, 28 although some of these were rolled back by the Phase One deal 
and afterward. 29 China also considered export restrictions on critical materials (e.g., rare earth minerals that

Bridging the Gap
66
shifted investment in technology firms from those in the United States to those in other advanced, non-U.S. 
countries.33
The actions taken by both China and the United States pointed in the direction of technological “decou -
pling,” under which supply chains and technological R&D would occur independently in each country. Pos -
sible implications include products developed in one country being excluded for sale in the other, a diver -
gence in technology standards, and a loss of China-based revenue for U.S. firms based in China. Decoupling 
is likely to be enormously expensive for both countries, and to date, both sides have been cautious of fully 
embracing the possibility.
Responses to Increased U.S.-China Competition
This section explores how the U.S. technology sector responded to U.S. and PRC economic actions. We ana-
lyze the consequences of U.S. and Chinese policy actions across the hardware and software subsectors sepa -
rately, as the reactions may vary between them.
Major Adjustments or Changes in Business Activities
We look at changes in U.S. technology sector activities across five main business functions: operations, sales 
and marketing, finance, R&D, and employment and human resources, as well as changes since the onset of 
COVID-19. 
Operations
The hardware portion of the technology sector showed little inclination to support overall U.S. policy actions 
toward China during the Trump administration. While the subsector may be broadly sympathetic to the 
larger goal of fair trade practices and IP rights, it was similar to the manufacturing sector in that there were 
large concerns about how increased U.S.-China competition would threaten supply chains, increase costs, 
and reduce market access to the large Chinese domestic market.
34
Reticence to support U.S. policy has manifested from those concerns. For example, when the Trump 
administration proposed placing tariffs on electronic goods, hardware firms such as Apple and HP replied 
that such tariffs would harm their business by making their products less affordable for American con -
sumers. Although the tariffs incentivized supply chain relocation, they noted that this could not be easily 
achieved in the short term. Supply chain relocation would decrease their revenue by making their products 
more expensive for consumers, largely benefiting non-U.S. competitors, which do not tend to have large U.S. 
market share and consequently would not see their costs increase because of the tariffs. If firms had to pay 
for supply chain relocation, this would reduce the funding available to maintain R&D and America’s lead in 
innovation.
35
But even if the supply chain were to be reconfigured, technology firms may not be able to completely 
remove Chinese production. In exchange for market access in China, which firms highly desire, the Chinese 
33 Yuan Yang, “U.S. Tech Backlash Forces China to Be More Self-Sufficient,” Financial Times , January 15, 2020.
34 Devi Keller (on behalf of SIA), comment to proposed rule (USTR-2019-0004-1743), June 16, 2019, p. 1; Mike Russo (on 
behalf of SEMI), comment to proposed rule (USTR-2019-0004-0124), May 29, 2019, p. 1.

posted on June 19, 2019e.

authorities often require at least some domestic production. 36 In addition, as one executive with experience 
in venture capital pointed out, “low labor costs in China are no longer an attraction. What they offer is scale 
leverage [that] is very hard to replicate elsewhere. [China] became much better at producing higher-end 
stuff.” As HP pointed out, companies often single-source or mostly source from China:
Currently, China is the single source for HP laptop production; any changes in sourcing aimed to mitigate 
tariff impacts would be costly. 
China has the most mature ecosystem for laptop manufacturing in the world. This ecosystem has evolved 
over decades through the steady investment of U.S., Japanese, and Taiwanese companies across the supply 
chain. China is currently the source of over 90% of laptop and tablet units imported to [sic] the U.S. China’s 
volume of supply is 42 times greater than the next largest supplier—Taiwan.

as certain critical components can be made only in China, such as [ ].
37 
On the software side, the production of software does not generally require Chinese involvement. Soft -
ware companies, however, may have R&D facilities on the mainland, but in our interviews, many executives 
reported that these facilities tended to not be of principal importance and may work on matters such as 
adapting existing products for the Chinese market.
38 Consequently, the concerns about supply chains do not 
apply here and the operations of software companies were less affected.
Sales and Marketing
Hardware firms were wary of tensions that would suppress profits that could be used to fund research and 
maintain or bolster the competitiveness of their products (or be paid out to owners). In the case of the tariffs 
on Chinese imports, the semiconductor industry expressed concerns about increased costs, due in large part 
to the structure of the semiconductor supply chain. According to the Semiconductor Industry Association 
(SIA), a typical semiconductor is constructed in seven stages.
39 Most of the manufacturing is done in Japan, 
Malaysia, Singapore, or the United States, with final assembly of the chip done in China. Although SIA 
asserts that the final assembly in China is a cost-effective step, it is relatively low in value added.
Consequently, the hardware industry feared that any increased China-U.S. competition may threaten this 
final assembly, raising prices while not necessary protecting their IP. This is evident in their public comments 
in the USTR’s proposal to raise tariffs on semiconductor products. The industry noted that as a result of the 
tariffs, vendors would have to raise prices, reducing demand for products and cutting profit margins; this 
would result in a smaller American IT market by $70 billion over 2019–2020 and “costs to the U.S. economy 
and IT sector in the form of cutbacks in IT spending, job losses and weaker wage growth as companies face 
slowing profitability and revenue.” It feared that this would be followed by knock-on effects such as reduced 
36 This may violate WTO rules. See U.S. International Trade Administration, “Trade Guide: WTO Agreement on Trade-
Related Investment Measures,” webpage, undated b.
37 Regulations.gov, 2019e, p. 4; the square brackets represent business-confidential information that was redacted from the 
public USTR filings.
38 Telephone interview with industry group representative, July 7, 2020 (name withheld on request).
39 Nathan Associates, Beyond Borders: The Global Semiconductor Value Chain , Washington, D.C.: Semiconductor Industry 
Association, May 2016, p. 10.

68
investment in new technologies and that increased prices would reduce their competitive ability relative to 
Chinese firms. 40
For software firms, the response to increased U.S.-China competition has been relatively muted because 
this subsector differs from the hardware subsector. Most of this subsector is not as exposed to China econom-
ically. According to FactSet, of the top 23 firms with the highest percentage of revenue in China, only one is 
a software company and it is a financial technology (fintech) company: Mastercard. The prospects for many 
software firms are not particularly good; in fact, many have tried and failed to enter the Chinese market. 
Companies such as Amazon, Facebook, and Google have tried to operate in the Chinese market but left, 
finding the operational burdens (such as those related to censorship) too large or finding themselves unable 
to make significant headway into the economy.
41 Others, such as Microsoft, have refused to offer services 
such as email to Chinese customers on human rights principles. 42 Meanwhile, China has promoted its own 
national champions in the form of Alibaba, Baidu, and Renren, raising the barrier to entry for any would-be 
U.S. firms. Consequently, most of these firms have little revenue at stake in China.
It is important to point out that U.S. fintech companies have a more optimistic view of prospects in China 
compared with more traditional software firms. Similar to financial firms that view the China market as a 
potential growth opportunity (for more detailed discussion, see Chapter Eight), fintech firms benefited from 
more access in China during the Trump administration. For example, in September 2019 PayPal became the 
first foreign payment platform licensed for services in China, ending a monopoly by the state-owned Union-
Pay.
43 Similarly, as of mid-2020, Mastercard and Visa, the world’s largest payment companies, were closer to 
getting approvals to operate in mainland China. 44
U.S. fintech companies, however, obtained market access after Chinese companies were well-established, 
inviting questions as to how much they can benefit from such increased access. For example, while market 
barriers initially prevented U.S. e-commerce companies from competing in this emerging sector in 2013, by 
the time they were lowered in 2015, “major Chinese e-commerce companies [e.g., state-owned UnionPay] 
had established highly integrated platforms and payment systems linked to local social media giants and 
gained the loyalty of the Chinese customer base, making it nearly impossible for foreign companies to get 
a share of the market.”
45 In the case of traditional payment processing credit card companies like Master -
card and Visa, the Chinese government allowed them to operate in China only after domestic mobile pay -
ment companies had occupied the lion’s share of the payments market and had nothing to fear from foreign 
competitors.
46 Since 2013, PRC e-commerce companies such as Ant Financial, Alibaba, and Tencent have

Research and Development
In contrast to its decline in the manufacturing sector, R&D spending continued to increase for the technol-
ogy sector from 2010 to 2019 (see Figure 7.3). It is not clear if U.S.-China tensions contributed to this trend. 
But evidence might suggest that tensions may have had little effect because R&D was already decoupled. 
Very few U.S. companies, whether software or hardware, have important R&D offices in China. Due to IP 
concerns, limited market access, and the unlevel playing field, an American Chamber of Commerce survey 
reported that fewer than 4 percent of American companies deem their China offices their most important 
R&D hubs. The majority believed their Chinese R&D operations were of “minimal importance” or just one 
of several innovation hubs.
51
Employment and Human Resources
For the most part, employment in the technology sector has been stable since 2017. Hardware companies 
increased employment from 1.04 million jobs in December 2016 to 1.10 million in December 2019, while soft-
ware companies increased employment from 1.38 million to 1.40 million jobs over the same time.
52 Because 
employment is affected by various factors, including those not related to U.S.-China competition, it is not 
readily apparent if U.S.-China tensions had a significant effect on overall technology sector employment 
levels.
A new development that may have affected corporate activities was the difficulty of obtaining U.S. visas. 
For example, the suspension of H-1B visa issue between June and the end of 2020 likely deterred hiring 
skilled foreign workers that could contribute to the U.S. technology sector’s innovative capability. This fol-

may pose barriers for U.S. innovation. Because U.S. companies do little of their R&D in China, they usually 
sponsor talented Chinese engineers with H-1B visas. Chinese nationals, however, only account for 10 percent 
of H-1B visa holders as of 2017.
53 It is possible that restricting visas may further reduce the number of skilled 
Chinese nationals working in the U.S. technology sector, given the trend of more scientists of Chinese origin 
returning to China.
54 
Changes Since the Onset of COVID-19
Since 2017, U.S. businesses have thought about localizing production near consumption. Functionally, that 
means that production may move out of China, but not necessarily home to the United States; exports to 
Asian countries may be made in China, while exports destined for the United States would be made in North 
America. Companies such as Apple and HP have weighed whether to shift as much as 30 percent of their 
China-based production to such places as India, the Philippines, Taiwan, and Vietnam.
55 More generally, 
across the technology sector as a whole, the Washington Post reported that a July 2019 survey from QIMA 
“showed that demand for China-based [quality control] inspections from U.S. companies had fallen 13 per -
cent in the first half of 2019. In the same window, overall demand for inspections in South Asia jumped 
34 percent.”
56 According to a report from Kearney, for U.S. manufacturing and technology sectors (as defined 
by this report), the shift out of China represents about $90 billion in imported goods. 57
The emergence of the COVID-19 pandemic is likely to accelerate much of these trends and, in particular, 
supply chain relocation. The pandemic has brought up much talk of “supply chain resilience” and the dangers 
of overly relying on a single country for production. As a result, supply chains are likely to shift to reduce 
reliance, but the extent to which this phenomenon will occur is unknown. While building a resilient supply 
chain will increase security of supply, it will also incur costs such as excess inventory and capital expenditure 
to build factories that tie up shareholder capital without necessarily generating immediate value.
Responses to Shape U.S. Policies or PRC Responses
Although the hardware industry was sympathetic to broader concerns in the U.S.-China relationship (e.g., IP 
theft), it took clear public positions against the administration’s actions. It appears that these concerns were 
driven in large part by economic considerations. An executive at a venture capital fund pointed out that prof-
its were a principal concern and that “[m]any [firms] are saying that will continue working with China and 
[will find ways to] circumvent U.S. restrictions. Companies have always done this
 . . . 
 as w
as the case with 
selling telecom equipment in Brazil. People are incentivized to figure out workarounds.” 58 In response to 
some export controls, an executive at a U.S. company worried that “[s]ecuring new clients in China, or even

As in the manufacturing sector, we identified very little support for Section 301 tariffs (see Table 7.1). 
Companies may sympathize with broader concerns that U.S. policymakers are trying to address, but rarely 
did companies think tariffs were the best means of achieving such an objective. The concerns generally relate 
to increased prices for consumers, decreasing the amount of profit and investment capacity. 
The lack of support for Trump administration measures against China extended to other forms of policy 
actions. Some of the resistance was relatively benign. For example, the Business Roundtable opposed the ICTS 
rule, deeming it “unworkable for U.S. businesses” and writing that it would create “uncertainty and confu -
sion that could undermine both U.S. competitiveness and national security objectives” because “[f]oreign 
partners will do less business with U.S. companies.”
60 Other responses to U.S. policies were more aggres -
sive, as in the case with Huawei. Although U.S. companies were banned from exporting U.S. technology to 
Huawei as early as May 2019, some U.S. companies shifted tactics by making products outside the United 
States (products produced overseas are not always considered made in America) and then selling the prod -
ucts to Huawei.

what type of policy support to use. HP noted that it already “implemented practices that are more effective
