at remedying the problematic Chinese IP practice.” In lieu of the aforementioned tariffs, it preferred Section 
337 actions, in which HP would bring IP-related disputes to the International Trade Commission, seeking 
enforcement actions such as keeping out infringing imported goods:
Section 337 actions enjoy high success rates and are effective because they completely exclude infring -
ing products from the U.S. market. A broad-based tariff, by contrast, is a less effective tool since it would 
still allow infringing products into the U.S. market, would not distinguish between legitimate and ille -
gitimate goods and would indiscriminately raise prices for all customers. A number of printer technol -
ogy companies, including HP, Canon, and Epson, have used Section 337 actions successfully in recent 
years.
63 
In the case of the software industry, which has a relative lack of economic interests in China, companies 
have tried to maintain neutrality in the U.S.-China competition; Microsoft even declared itself a “neutral 
digital Switzerland.” Several reasons potentially motivate this desire to maintain neutral. First, as a former 
policy adviser at Google pointed out, the companies continue to hold out hope that they can operate in China 
to maximize profits.
64 Second, there exist some cultural and human resource concerns. As an executive with 
strong connections to Silicon Valley pointed out, “The industry has a strong ‘Silicon Valley bias’—people 
don’t care about the government and are focused on their own business. There is an arrogant view that D.C. is 
focused on solving ‘yesterday’s problems.’
 ”65 Additionally, Microsoft president Brad Smith noted that “[there 
has been an] explosion of innovation in China” and that what “we at Microsoft have long appreciated is the 
enormous ingenuity of the engineering population of China.” In fact, Smith argued that basic research on 
topics like machine learning and quantum computing must be published for the sake of scientific advance -
ment.
66 Microsoft founder Bill Gates further deplored the “paranoid” view inherent in the technology ten -
sions between China and the United States, saying that trying to stop Beijing from developing innovative 
technologies is “beyond realistic.”
67 
Compared to the hardware sector, it is possible that the software sector could benefit to some degree from 
increased U.S.-China competition for several reasons. First, competition could create growth potential for 
some software companies. U.S. software companies could benefit from limits on the use and sales of PRC 
software products in the United States. U.S.-China tensions could also create demand for specific America-
made technology services (particularly for security or military use) and increase U.S. federal contracts. In 
fact, such contracts have been growing at a rapid pace. The Pentagon’s spending on “Command, Control, 
Communications, Computers, and Intelligence” systems, which include IT spending, has grown 60 percent 
since 2016, from $7.4 billion to $11.9 billion.
68 This has resulted in notable contracts. Recently, the Pentagon 
offered Microsoft a $10 billion Joint Enterprise Defense Infrastructure contract to build a cloud computing 
service.
69 This represents a substantial increase from the $259 million in federal contracts that Microsoft

74
received in 2016 and the $459 million offered in 2019.70 Additionally, exclusion of Chinese companies operat-
ing in the United States may produce an opportunity for domestic companies to expand further.
Second, technology companies could use competition with China to obtain more U.S. government or 
public support. For example, Facebook is the subject of an antitrust investigation. 71 Since it is currently 
blocked in China and is unlikely to see any significant near-term business opportunity there (which requires 
setting up a local subsidiary subject to censorship, a violation of Facebook’s stated norms), the company’s 
chief executive officer, Mark Zuckerberg, appealed to U.S. patriotism to defend the company, suggesting that 
if Facebook’s development is hindered, then it will be unable to push the forefront of key national priorities 
such as AI, perhaps surrendering this field to China.
72 The wielding of U.S.-China tensions by technology 
giants could grow in appeal in response to antitrust concerns, especially as other technology companies (e.g., 
Amazon, Google) face similar situations.
73
Although federal contracts and barriers to entry to the U.S. market may benefit some U.S. technology 
firms, technology executives are broadly worried about “techno-nationalism”—which, as one executive 
defined it, was a process through which each country would erect its own barriers to support its own domes-
tic technology firms and harm the prospects of American technology firms seeking to provide to a global 
market. For example, the executive pointed out that forcing TikTok to localize its American data in the 
United States may give a convenient excuse for other countries to force technology firms operating in their 
territories to localize their data and incentivize the development of competitors in areas where none exist 
(e.g., data localization in Europe may help the GAIA-X project, which seeks to foster “digital sovereignty” 
for Europe).
74 In our discussions, the fear of “techno-nationalism” was palpable, sufficient to offset any gains 
made possible by U.S.-China tensions to select U.S. technology firms. For this reason, any policy that could 
generate techno-nationalism is unlikely to be broadly supported by U.S. technology firms. 
Overall Effect of Increased U.S.-China Competition on the Sector 
Overall, evidence suggests that there was limited support, if not opposition, in the technology sector for 
Trump administration measures to confront China economically and technologically. In the hardware space, 
companies are too entangled in terms of production and a desire to sell in the Chinese market to want any 
friction that threatens those interests. U.S. policy actions negatively affected their business model; export 
controls and tariffs increased the cost of business and decreased their profit margins. The upside for U.S. pol-
icymakers is that if decoupling occurs, U.S. firms’ economic interest in China will likely decrease, lowering 
the incentive to go against U.S. government policy. The extent to which the interest will decrease and whether 
that will be enough to bolster support for U.S. government initiatives, however, is unknown.
Likewise, support in the software sector is likely to be weak, but for different reasons. Unlike the hardware 
sector, the software sector is less exposed to China in terms of sales and supply chain considerations. Even if 
they seek to expand their operations abroad, it is unclear whether such endeavors will necessarily be fruitful.

Views from the Technology Sector
75
U.S.-China tensions could open pockets of additional market opportunities—for example, additional federal 
contracts and filling the void left by PRC companies banned from operations in the United States. This, how-
ever, is unlikely to be enough of an incentive for companies to support greater confrontation with China. For 
one, federal contracts are unlikely to rival the global market in size. More saliently, companies are worried 
about the rise of techno-nationalism that would depress their prospects abroad. Additionally, support is still 
constrained by cultural factors such as workers refusing to help government agencies; for example, Microsoft 
workers protested a data analysis contract with U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement,
75 and Google 
employees protested helping the Pentagon improve drone strikes.76
Table 7.2 shows that, in general, increased U.S.-China competition has had an overall negative effect on 
the business of the technology sector—imposing more costs than conferring benefits across the five main 
business functions: operations, sales and marketing, finance, R&D, and employment and human resources. 
Absent any changes to economic incentives, support for U.S. policies against China might increase if more 
targeted policies (instead of broader actions such as across-the-board tariffs) were implemented. Addition -
ally, the business community strongly encourages working with key U.S. allies and partners on any measures; 
an executive at a large business organization pointed out that if export controls were unilateral, China might 
still be able to buy the end product, with the only result being that American companies would be displaced 
from making sales.

CHAPTER EIGHT
Views from the Financial Sector
The United States is home to the largest, most liquid, and most sophisticated financial markets in the world.1 
In 2019 the U.S. financial and insurance sector represented 7.6 percent of real GDP and accounted for about 
4.3 percent of total nonfarm U.S. employment. 2 Like the manufacturing and technology sectors, the U.S. 
financial sector is diverse. For simplicity, our analysis will group these types of firms indexed on the S&P 
500 into four subsectors: asset managers and global banks, regional banks, insurance firms, and diversified 
finance. 
The first category includes large asset managers like BlackRock, Fidelity, and Invesco, and diversified 
banks and investment banks like Citigroup, Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase, and Morgan Stanley. Exam -
ples of the 11 regional banks in the S&P 500 include the Citizens Financial Group, Fifth Third Bancorp, PNC 
Financial Services, and SVB Financial Group. The nearly two dozen insurance firms indexed in the S&P 500 
include industry giants like Aflac, Allstate, MetLife, Progressive, and State Farm. The last category, diversi -
fied finance, consists of a broad range of financial services corporations like American Express, Capital One 
Financial, and Discover, as well as financial exchanges like CME Financial Services and credit rating agencies 
and financial data providers like Moody’s and S&P Global. The following sections examine the U.S. financial 
sector’s views of U.S.-China competition.
Background: Operations and Involvement in China 
The Chinese financial system has experienced a historic period of opening to foreign firms and capital, par -
ticularly since 2018. However, despite significant progress made toward market liberalization and the cou -
pling of the U.S. and Chinese financial systems, the PRC still maintains one of the most restrictive foreign 
investment environments in the world for the U.S. banking and financial sector. 
The slow process of U.S.-China financial integration dates back to 1973, enabled by President Richard 
Nixon’s Cold War foreign policy reorientation toward rapprochement with China, when Chase Manhattan 
Bank became the first U.S. correspondent to the state-owned People’s Bank of China since the Communist 
1 For example, as of 2018 the U.S. banking system had $17.9 trillion in assets; U.S. asset managers held nearly $51 trillion 
in mutual funds, retirement assets, and insurance assets; U.S. insurers’ net premiums written totaled $1.1 trillion; U.S. 
private equity firms had over $500 billion invested domestically; and U.S. venture capitalists had some $84 billion invested 
globally (2017). SelectUSA, “Financial Services Spotlight: The Financial Services Industry in the United States,” website, 
undated. 
2 The financial and insurance sectors contributed $1.63 trillion to total real GDP in 2019 ($21.4 trillion); BEA, 2020b. In 2019 
the financial and insurance sectors averaged 6.4 million jobs monthly, compared with the total nonfarm monthly average of 
150.9 million jobs; BLS, 2018.

78
Party’s revolutionary victory in 1949. 3 Yet, until just a few decades ago, the PRC had virtually no private 
financial sector to speak of, as nearly all banking operations were conducted by enterprises of the state. 4 
U.S. financial companies began to slowly penetrate Chinese financial markets in the 1990s. 5 The first 
business ventures were primarily limited to cooperation with the Chinese government, SOEs, and state-
owned banks, the primary capital holders in the early years of market-oriented reforms. Since then, capital 
controls and barriers to foreign investment in domestic financial companies have remained the defining fac-
tors limiting the growth of U.S. participation in China’s massive financial markets despite long-standing PRC 
pledges to remove barriers to entry and level the competitive field for foreign firms.
6 
China’s financial industry is composed of a diverse range of institutions—fintech companies, brokerages, 
trust companies, and financial leasing companies—but it remains overwhelmingly dominated by commer -
cial banks. Commercial banks provide nearly 70 percent of all credit to the economy and account for more 
than 80 percent of total assets held by domestic financial institutions. In the decade following the 2008–2009 
global financial crisis, assets in China’s banking sector quadrupled from $10.2 trillion to $41.6 trillion as its 
commercial banks expanded their balance sheets and extended massive numbers of loans to companies and 
local governments.
7 The value of China’s financial markets has grown to the equivalent of roughly half of 
global GDP.8 
Mainland China’s financial industry remains, however, relatively underdeveloped and the banking system 
has recently come under significant pressure to clean up its balance sheets and dispose of nonperforming 
loans generated during the last decade of rapid expansion.
9 The economic fallout of the COVID-19 pandemic 
exacerbated these concerns “about a buildup of hidden risks on lenders’ balance sheets” as Chinese banks 
have reportedly taken “extraordinary [new] measures to avoid recognizing bad loans”—in a reversal of recent 
progress made to improve transparency and strengthen regulation in the rapidly-expanded financial indus -
try.
10 Furthermore, during this historic decade of credit and debt growth, a “vast amount of investment” was

directed toward the real estate market rather than more diversified financial products, leading to persistent 
speculation and fears of a looming correction in China’s property bubble.11 These trends have all contributed 
to the recent acceleration of China’s opening to foreign capital.12 
To these ends, in April 2018, Chinese Communist Party general secretary and PRC president Xi Jinping 
and People’s Bank of China governor Yi Gang announced that the PRC would accelerate its financial opening 
with historic policy actions designed to “(1) increase market access in the banking, securities, and insurance 
industries; (2) grant foreign institutions equal treatment in credit and payment sectors; and (3) open up the 
domestic bond market to foreign investors.”
13 Beginning in 2018 as the U.S.-China trade war accelerated, Bei-

mutual fund firms, and futures companies—eventually giving foreign companies a path to obtain full own -
ership.
14 In June 2018, Chinese regulators further agreed to raise “foreign equity caps on banking, securities, 
and insurance joint ventures to 51 percent”—and to eventually remove them entirely by 2020.15 In 2018–2019, 
the PRC also continued to pursue the opening of its stock and bond markets to attract new foreign capital by 
launching the long-awaited Shanghai-London Stock Connect and by removing limits on foreign holdings in 
Chinese banks. (Prior to these reforms, individual and group foreign institutions were limited to 20 percent 
and 25 percent holding stakes in Chinese banks, respectively.)
16 
Despite such progress in opening to foreign competition, U.S. financial firms listed in the S&P 500 Index 
have not been significantly dependent on revenues from China (see Figure 8.1). Based on our analysis of 
66 financial firms in the S&P 500 and financial data provided by FactSet, the average firm exposure to the 
Chinese market was only about 1.2 percent of global revenue, with no firms relying on China for more than 
10 percent of their global revenue. Only six firms (four insurers and two asset managers) received more than 
5 percent of their total revenue from China, while China accounted for between 0 and 5 percent of global 
revenue for about one-third of U.S. financial firms in the S&P 500.
17 Significantly, nearly six in ten U.S. 
financial firms listed in the S&P 500 had no reported revenues in the PRC in 2019. This includes 100 percent 
of regional banks, 58 percent of asset managers and global banks, 52 percent of insurance companies, and 
38 percent of diversified finance firms.

lacks robust mechanisms for pricing risk. Chinese policymakers know this and would like to harness foreign expertise to 
professionalize the financial sector and find innovative solutions to current problems. Although the moderate expansion of 
market access to China’s financial markets over the last two years stems in part from a need for capital, stresses in the bank -
ing sector do underpin legitimate demand for the services of foreign ratings agencies, insurers, fund managers, and distressed

As of 2020, most of the largest U.S. financial firms in the class of asset managers, diversified banks, and 
investment banks had set up offices in China and were offering China-related products to their clients. In this 
financial firm category, at least 12 companies out of 19 in our S&P 500 Index sample had offices in mainland 
China. The picture is similar for diversified financial firms (at least eight out of 13 had operations in China) 
and insurance companies (at least 11 out of 22). U.S. regional banks, in general, did not have significant 
operations in China and did not compete with Chinese banks for shares of the commercial banking market. 
Instead their presence was often limited to small offices that typically did not offer any core products or ser-
vices and appeared to have mainly a consulting role.
18
China is also a modest destination for FDI for the U.S. financial sector. In 2019 the U.S. financial and 
insurance industry (excepting depository institutions) held $9.6 billion in FDI in China.19 Depository institu-
tions accounted for an additional $4.95 billion of U.S. FDI in China in 2019.20 Taken together as an economic 
sector, U.S. financial, insurance, and depository institutions thus accounted for $14.6 billion (12.6 percent) of 
America’s total FDI position in China at the start of 2020 ($116.2 billion).

the foreign investment position of the Chinese financial sector in the United States is significantly smaller. 22 
In 2019, Chinese financial, insurance, and depository institutions together accounted for just $3.1 billion, or 
8 percent of total PRC investment in the United States. This $3.1 billion from China made up only 0.4 percent 
of total FDI stock (from all countries) in these sectors in the United States ($763.3 billion).
23 
Beyond bilateral FDI, U.S. policy toward the PRC could also affect U.S. financial exchanges, which list 
Chinese companies. Notably, between September 2019 and October 2020, the number of Chinese companies 
listed and raising capital on the U.S. financial market’s three biggest exchanges—Nasdaq, the New York Stock 
Exchange (NYSE), and the NYSE American—increased from 172 to 217, including 13 national-level Chinese 
SOEs. At the same time, these firms’ total market capitalization more than doubled, from about $1 trillion 
to over $2.2 trillion.
24 
Views of Competition from China and China-Related Operations 
U.S. financial firms remain largely excluded from the Chinese domestic market due to a variety of regula -
tory restrictions, such as negative investment lists and ownership caps, and a host of other distortionary and 
protectionist laws and practices designed to advantage state-owned and private domestic firms over foreign 
competition.
25 For decades, the U.S. financial sector has actively lobbied and negotiated for policy changes 
in three areas: (1) full ownership and operating control of their operations in China, (2) greater market 
access, and (3) general relief on various regulatory issues.
26 To these ends, actions taken by Beijing since 2018 
(described in the section “Background: Operations and Involvement in China” in this chapter) have gener -
ally been viewed by the U.S. financial industry as a step in the right direction toward liberalization—albeit 
as a still plodding progress. The PRC’s policy changes have opened new business opportunities and enabled 
many major American players to take control of existing JVs—even if business leaders remain skeptical about 
their implementation in practice and wary of remaining formal and informal competitive disadvantages and 
barriers to doing business in China.
27 For example, in a recent survey by the American Chamber of Com -
merce in China, 74 percent of respondents said that the environment is improving in the financial services 
sector (e.g., banking, insurance), while 100 percent said it was improving in investing (e.g., private equity and 
venture capital).
28
Notably, the U.S. and Chinese financial industries’ drift toward deeper integration is generally viewed 
as mutually beneficial to both national interests and as generally unmarred by the direct national security 
implications implicit in the trade wars and IP espionage battles dominating U.S.-China competition in the

In short, given China’s professed commitment to opening up and its desire to attract foreign capital—as 
well as broader macroeconomic fundamentals such as continued Chinese economic growth; an expanding 
middle class; increasing demand for equity and debt products, insurance, credit ratings and risk pricing, 
and other asset management and financial services—industry representatives interviewed generally con -

83
At the same time, U.S. financial institutions seeking to operate in China also “know better by now than to 
assume that the economy’s scale will directly translate into business for them,” 39 and past efforts “to encour-
age China to adopt market-oriented reforms have had little success.”40 U.S. financial firms understand that a 
range of factors could continue to disadvantage their ability to compete. As one interviewee noted, it is very 
“institution-specific” as to which chief executive officers are “very long China,” and which are less so. 41 On 
the regulatory front, significant challenges remain despite the momentum in financial integration that was 
achieved through 2020.
42 Beyond regulatory challenges, structural legacies will prohibit market penetration 
for decades or longer regardless of market liberalization. Chinese banks account for over three-fourths of all 
assets in China’s financial system, and the government maintains a controlling stake in China’s largest five 
banks and significant stakes in countless second-tier commercial banks.
43 U.S. financial firms understand 
that they will not likely be able to “compete for deposits against the likes of Industrial and Commercial Bank 
of China, which boasts some 15,700 branches”—but they may be able to compete for significantly larger 
market shares in asset management and investment banking, where success is “more related to experience 
than sheer heft.”
44 
According to an analysis by Deloitte in the insurance subsector, some 50 foreign firms have entered the 
market since the 1990s, but as of the end of 2018, the largest Sino-foreign JVs accounted for just 1.3 percent 
and 0.5 percent of the life insurance and non-life-insurance market shares, respectively, while the top 15 
firms in each market segment remained dominated by Chinese-owned enterprises.
45 
Finally, U.S. financial institutions generally do not view Chinese banks and financial companies as 
major competitors outside China. Instead, leaders in the U.S. financial industry expressed the outlook that 
their know-how, deep capital bases, expertise, and global reach place them in a position that is unlikely 
to be challenged by Chinese financial institutions in the foreseeable future.

Major U.S. Policy Actions or PRC Responses That Affected the Sector 
While the most high-profile U.S. economic actions against China (and corresponding PRC responses) since 
2017 have been concentrated in the manufacturing and technology sectors, the U.S. federal government has 
also undertaken a series of direct and indirect measures designed to restrict investments by U.S. financial 
firms and limit new operations in China.
First, in May 2020 the Trump administration directed the U.S. government’s Thrift Savings Plan, which 
oversees some $600 billion in federal retirement dollars, to cancel a plan that would allow investment in 
Chinse stocks, citing inadequate disclosures and China’s handling of the COVID-19 virus.
48 Previously, in 
2017, the Federal Retirement Thrift Investment Board had decided that the Thrift Savings Plan’s $40 bil -
lion international fund would “begin tracking an index that includes Chinese companies”—an invest -
ment shift “hailed by unions” seeking to diversify and boost returns but maligned by China hawks who 
argue that U.S. federal pension savings should not be invested in dual civil-military Chinese firms, some 
of which have been sanctioned for human rights abuses.
49 More broadly, index providers have generally 
increased the weighting of Chinese stocks in American investment portfolios like 401(k)s and other retire -
ment vehicles, amounting to a major win for the PRC by funneling U.S. capital into the Chinese market 
and strengthening the renminbi. As one industry analyst recently remarked, “For China, this is the great -
est free lunch program for capital they’ve ever known, because they’re able to penetrate the investment 
portfolios of scores of millions of Americans in one shot.” In the view of some leaders of the U.S. financial 
industry, such U.S. policy actions “to restrict investment constitute government interference and could 
destabilize financial markets” as an unintended consequence of market interventions to weaken U.S.-
China financial linkages.
50
Second, as discussed in the preceding chapters, the DOC has also imposed new sanctions regimes against 
dozens of Chinese private and state-owned firms (as well as some senior Chinese officials) over human rights 
violations in Xinjiang (in October 2019 and June 2020);
51 the PRC’s actions to undermine the autonomy of 
and political freedoms in Hong Kong (in July 2020);52 the PRC’s militarization of islands in the disputed terri-

torial waters of the South China Sea (in August 2020);53 and national security concerns in advanced computer 
chips, semiconductors, and other high-tech sectors. 54 While these sanctions were not primarily directed 
against financial firms, they nevertheless complicated the compliance efforts of any U.S. financial firms that 
have had “significant transactions” with these entities in the past, while introducing “uncertainty for inter -
national banks that were looking at a historic opening of the Chinese financial market.” 55 
In interviews with RAND researchers, some representatives of the U.S. financial industry expressed par-
ticular frustration with the DOC’s interactions with the business community to explain and get feedback on 
China-related actions since the start of the trade war (2018)—at least relative to the business community’s 
historical experience interacting with State Department and Treasury officials on similar enforcement issues 
at the nexus of public- and private-sector interests.
56 These individuals credited the State Department and 
Treasury’s longer experience in designing and implementing sanctions regimes compared with the DOC, 
which historically has had far less institutional practice in sanctions design and enforcement. U.S. financial 
leaders expressed particular concerns about uncertainty over sanctions enforcement related to individuals 
and entities vaguely deemed to be “involved in the erosion of certain obligations of China with respect to 
Hong Kong” under the Hong Kong Autonomy Act of 2020.
57 For example, could a major U.S. financial firm 
seeking to, say, expand its controlling share in a JV in Shanghai be penalized if the PRC regulator handling 
its case ended up on the list of sanctioned individuals? Further, U.S. financial executives indicated that while 
industry compliance programs are fairly robust at first-level screenings of known, sanctioned individuals 
and entities, enforcement of bad actors becomes far more difficult at shadowy secondary levels of screening 
(e.g., shell companies and cryptocurrency financial transactions)—injecting strategic risk to expansion of 
their business models in Chinese markets due to U.S. policy uncertainty and U.S. sanctions opaqueness.
58 
To better protect the U.S. financial industry and private investors, the U.S. executive branch and Congress 
have been discussing a variety of other policy measures ranging from strengthening audit and other regula -
tory scrutiny on Chinese companies selling shares on U.S. stock exchanges and delisting some Chinese firms 
from U.S. exchanges for a period of three years for disclosure and regulatory compliance failures to a broader 
campaign designed to persuade public and private investment firms to limit U.S. portfolio flows into China.
59

with China.

This section explores how the U.S. financial sector has responded to the PRC and U.S. economic policy 
actions. It concludes with reflections on the industry’s response to the COVID-19 pandemic through the lens 
of U.S.-China competition.

Major Adjustments or Changes in Business Activities
Here we analyze changes in U.S. financial activities across four main business functions: operations, sales 
and marketing, R&D, and employment and human resources. Unlike the previous chapters, however, we do 
not assess changes in finance. This is because much of the activities that we would have covered under the 
financial sector is discussed under the “Operations” section given that this chapter analyzes U.S. financial 
firms. 
Operations
Overall, recent policy developments, particularly since 2018, have had mixed consequences on the operations 
of American financial firms—although no industry representatives interviewed for this study reported that 
increased U.S.-China competition under the Trump administration had had a significant, adverse effect on 
their firms’ core operations in China. 
Rather, U.S. financial firms have continued to expand their operations in China despite the escalating 
trade war and other bilateral frictions. As shown in Figure 8.2, U.S. holdings of Chinese stocks and bonds 
have risen rapidly in recent years, outpacing holdings even in Hong Kong. Increasing exports of financial 
services to China and portfolio holdings of Chinese assets—together with a flurry of applications for busi -
ness expansion—suggest that, over the past few years, U.S. financial firms opted for greater participation in 
the Chinese financial market despite some frustration (and risk) associated with China’s alternative account-
ing practices and challenging developments on the legislative front.

“U.S.-China financial decoupling is not happening”; to the contrary, China’s integration into global markets 
“appears to have accelerated over the past year.”61
On the negative side, China’s aforementioned domestic policies on cybersecurity and national security 
have complicated the operations of all foreign financial firms’ JVs operating in China and have added mar-
ginal compliance costs due to new data localization and other national security legislation requirements.
62

systems.

protection concerns.

88
security laws could leave them vulnerable for prosecution. 66 Some business leaders also worried that Beijing 
could use the new law to force foreign banks to do business with U.S.-sanctioned individuals or firms, thus 
putting them in legal and political jeopardy. 67 
Other financial firms were concerned that the new law could be used to restrict critical research on pub -
licly traded enterprises with majority state ownership, since certain aspects of their financial performance 
can be regarded a “state secret.”
68 
On the more positive side, China agreed to increase access for U.S. financial firms as part of the Phase 
One deal, allowing U.S. companies to expand their operations in China.69 That is, despite the Trump admin-
istration’s trade wars and increased U.S.-China competition since 2018, industry experts said that most U.S. 
financial firms with JVs in China plan to increase these operations to 100 percent ownership—even though 
profit streams from most U.S. financial JVs have historically been relatively immaterial from an overall finan-
cial perspective.
70 As one industry leader noted, the primary motivation for most IP-based firms—including 
virtually all financial firms—with JVs in China is to maintain market access, not necessarily to compete for 
large profits from these start-ups’ market shares.
71 In other words, in the view of many U.S. financial firms, 
Chinese markets are a large source of volume, but not profits; market access is important for their global 
standing, but not their bottom line. That U.S. financial firms are willing to continue to operate unprofitably 
is equivalent to an options premium—although many foreign firms have lost money over the years by paying 
away those premiums.
72
At the time of the writing of this report, only a limited number of U.S. financial firms had already received 
the necessary approvals for full ownership of their JVs in China, while most others were still negotiating with 
their Chinese partners.
73 While a positive trend, the outcome of these ongoing negotiations remained highly 
uncertain, causing many companies to be concerned that geopolitical tensions could delay the approval of 
their applications or even halt the liberalization of the Chinese financial sector. That said, no U.S. financial 
industry representatives interviewed for this study could point to tangible, overt examples of political retalia-
tion or pressure applied to their firms’ operations in China—or to major changes in the constant bargaining 
between the PRC government and foreign financial firms—as a direct result of the trade war.
74 While it is 
difficult to assess the net effect of the U.S.-China competition on the operations of U.S. financial firms, we 
can still discuss some first- and second-order implications on the industry, subsector by subsector. 
First, it appears that the operations of U.S. regional banks were affected the least, since they usually do not 
operate subsidiaries or JVs in China. That said, growing U.S.-China competition and the imposition of tariffs 
in the escalating trade war have broadly increased uncertainty in financial markets and caused equity prices 
to fall, creating second-order effects throughout the entire U.S. financial sector.

Views from the Financial Sector
89
Second, the effect of increased U.S.-China competition on asset managers and large diversified banks has 
been relatively positive. On the one hand, they have been negatively affected by the market-wide macroeffects 
of the trade war (except in the case of traders who were successful in taking advantage of resulting market 
volatility). On the other hand, those firms that have operations in (or intend to expand into) China have ben-
efited from increased market access and accelerated approvals by Chinese regulators in the aftermath of the 
Phase One deal.
75 
Third, the operations of U.S. insurance companies were also set to benefit from increased access to China’s 
domestic market in the aftermath of the Phase One deal. China had promised “to remove business scope limi-
tations, discriminatory regulatory requirements, and overly burdensome licensing and operating require -
ments,” the most extensive such commitment of all financial subsectors. 76 As one Deloitte study concludes, 
the operations of U.S. insurers should benefit from three competitive changes in the Chinese market: (1) 
elimination of requirements mandating that foreign firms must have operated for at least 30 years before 
accessing China, (2) loosening of ownership caps to permit wholly foreign-owned firms, and (3) removal of 
rules and regulation on branch establishment by foreign insurers, which do not apply to Chinese firms.
77 
As of mid-2020, however, European and Asian insurance companies were the first foreign beneficiaries to 
receive approvals to set up wholly owned subsidiaries in China, followed by U.S. companies.
78 
Finally, the operations of other financial institutions grouped into “diversified finance” have also seen sig-
nificant improvements from China’s opening-up policies. In January 2020, for example, American Express 
became the “first foreign credit card company to launch onshore operations in China.” Similarly, in the credit 
rating services industry, in 2019 S&P Global became the first wholly foreign-owned firm “licensed to conduct

credit rating services in China’s domestic debt market” and in May 2020 Fitch’s established its first wholly 
foreign-owned subsidiary licensed “to rate China’s onshore issuers, including banks, nonbank financial insti-
tutions, and insurers, and their bonds.”79 
Although China’s financial opening benefited U.S. financial firms by expanding their operations in China, 
it is not clear that U.S. pressure or the range of actions taken by the Trump administration against China 
caused China to open up when it did. In late 2016, before the administration was in place, China’s National 
Development and Reform Commission had already revealed plans “to allow more foreign investment in bank-
ing, insurance, securities, and credit-rating firms,” although the extent and timeline for the change were not 
specified.
80 It is possible that China could have accelerated its liberalization plans to try to provide a “con -
cession” to the Trump administration. It is also possible that there was no acceleration on Beijing’s part and 
that China repackaged what it intended to do anyway into the Phase One trade deal, allowing the U.S. side to 
describe the financial opening as a “win” for the Trump administration. In RAND interviews, U.S. financial 
sector and industry experts expressed doubt that China would have agreed to open up its financial markets if it 
was not in China’s interest to do so and that the Trump administration could, at most, take credit for accelerat-
ing the pace of liberalization, although even that is up for debate.
81 As one interviewee commented, “Everyone 
knows what the end game is going to be—the only question is: How many years is it going to take to get it?”82 
With respect to Chinese investment in U.S. financial firms, there is some evidence to suggest that general 
increased U.S.-China tensions and measures the Trump administration took against China may have limited 
such PRC activities after 2017 (see Figure 8.3). Driven by a one-time jump in 2017, China engaged in levels 
of investment in the U.S. financial sector (a total of $17.5 billion) from 2015 onward similar to the levels of 
investment in the manufacturing ($16.2 billion) and technology ($12.5 billion) sectors. But in 2018 and 2019,

Sales and Marketing
Our analysis did not find strong evidence to suggest that increased U.S.-China competition during the Trump 
administration adversely affected U.S. financial firms’ sales and marketing activities. Aggregate exports of 
financial services by U.S. firms to China, for example, increased by over 18 percent between 2017 and 2019, 
from $4.0 billion to $4.8 billion.
83 Among large diversified banks, most S&P 500 firms in our sample saw an 
increase in sales to China between 2017 and 2019, with Bank of New York Mellon Corporation, Citigroup, 
and Morgan Stanley reporting the largest increases over the two-year period.
84 Most of the growth occurred 
in financial intermediation services, securities lending and electronic funds transfer, and other financial 
services (mainly comprised of underwriting and private placement and credit cards and other card-related 
services). However, as noted earlier, it is important to remember that most U.S. banks have a relatively low 
baseline exposure to the Chinese market and mostly view China as a source of future sales growth rather 
than current revenue.
85 
Asset managers also have a low exposure to the Chinese market in terms of global sales but increasingly 
view Chinese equity and debt as important elements in their global investment portfolios—and thus also an 
important component of their strategies to market new diversified products to boost sales and profits. This 
process is accelerating with the inclusion of Chinese bonds and equities into emerging market and global 
indexes, leading to a shift in strategic allocations toward greater exposure to Chinese assets.
86 In particular, 
the returns on Chinese debt instruments are looking more attractive relative to the returns in Europe and the 
United States, where the yields on government bonds are rather low given the current near-zero-interest-rate 
environment.
87 Most hedge funds also continued investing in Chinese assets looking for higher returns and 
diversification as a result of the accelerated competition between the two countries. Ray Dalio, the head of 
Bridgewater Associates, described a prevalent view that “China is a competitor of the United States or Chi -
nese businesses are competitors of American businesses or other business around the world, and that there -
fore you want to be, if you’re diversified, having bets on both horses in the race.”
88 
Perhaps more important, China remains the “largest untapped pool of capital in the world,” with over $28 
trillion in citizens’ bank deposits.89 This offers opportunities to foreign asset managers, who can significantly

increase their assets under management by establishing solo ventures or partnerships with local players. For 
example, Invesco’s Great Wall JV in China quadrupled its assets under management to $31.5 billion in less 
than a year after entering into a partnership with Ant Financial.
90 Global banks and asset managers are also 
exploring new revenue opportunities offered by China’s external economic activities—namely, the BRI. 91 
Insurance companies also increased their sales in China in 2019 relative to 2017. While data for individual 
companies show a mixed pattern, the overall premium income from foreign or Sino-foreign JV insurance 
companies increased by 29.9 percent year on year in 2019, double the growth rate of China’s domestic insur-
ers.
92 As with asset managers and large banks, the main focus of U.S. insurers appears to be on future growth 
potential of mainland China’s underdeveloped insurance market rather than current revenue streams. 93 For 
example, according to a recent analysis by Deloitte, China’s per capita insurance rate is only about 0.6 policies 
per person, compared with about 6.5 and 4.2 in Japan and the United States, respectively, and there have tra-
ditionally been far fewer product choices in China’s current insurance markets than in more developed ones, 
creating greater potential for market penetration by U.S. firms in this subsector of the financial industry than 
in others.
94 The fact that “sales channels and customer services are not well developed” in the Chinese market 
often results in “higher insurance coverage than customers’ risks may actually need”; new U.S. insurers may 
thus have competitive advantages in capturing segments of the growing middle class’s “demand for better 
insurance products and services.”
95
Notably, there was one important source of uncertainty that ran through several financial sector inter -
views: Industry leaders specifically voiced concerns about their ability to compete in China if future U.S. 
sanctions regimes were to target WeChat, the monopolistic Chinese-owned messaging and payment applica-
tion that is insulated by PRC state policy from global competitors like Facebook. At time of this writing, the 
Trump administration’s WeChat ban directly targeted only the app’s 19 million domestic users in the United 
States. But given cases pending in the federal court system, the future legal implications for U.S. financial 
firms desiring to conduct business in the PRC are much less clear.
96 Some U.S. financial industry executives 
we interviewed indicated that virtually all aspects of their local business model and client interface—such as

93
highly dependent on the WeChat platform and that U.S. government policies designed to mitigate national 
security risks posed by the app’s collection of data on American users could erode their ability to maintain 
and compete for new market share. Moreover, they questioned whether U.S. firms could be penalized for 
a financial transaction that originated in mainland China or Hong Kong via WeChat and then migrated 
into the U.S. financial system. Multiple industry leaders said that this regulatory uncertainty resulting from 
“ambiguous” and “opaque” U.S. government policy has had a strong, adverse impact on their business strat -

97 
Finally, despite the overall bias toward long investment positions and the general dislike of volatility that 
accompanies trade wars and geopolitical tensions, some financial firms have seen benefits from increasing 
volatility.
98 For example, financial exchanges have seen increased trading volumes as market participants 
tried to hedge their bets during the heights of the trade war. 99 Some hedge funds and investment funds also 
benefited from increased volatility and trading products like leveraged and inverse exchange-traded funds, 
which rise in value when markets go down.
100 Increased geopolitical tensions also expanded the market for 
certain insurance products, such as trade credit insurance and political risk insurance.
Research and Development
The research that financial companies do is not comparable to the R&D that manufacturing or technology 
firms engage in, and it is difficult to assess or quantify changes in R&D in the finance industry over time. 
That said, U.S. financial leaders interviewed generally concurred that the Trump administration’s economic 
policies toward China had no discernible industry-wide effect on investment in R&D activities.
101 As one 
interviewee explained, unlike R&D in industries such as petrochemicals, pharmaceuticals, and defense, most 
R&D value added in the financial industry lies in software development (i.e., adding layers of logic inside 
existing platform systems)—which has not been manifestly affected by the U.S. trade war.
102 That is, regard-
less of White House policies, Chinese rules require U.S. financial firms’ systems and software to be hosted in 
China, making overseas R&D prone to reverse engineering. Another voice from the fintech sector noted, for 
example, that the U.S. financial industry simply does not do much critical R&D in China due to the under -
lying IP and legal concerns.
103 Other interviewees similarly concurred that the issue of moving proprietary 
client and IP data offshore is generally less of a concern for U.S. financial firms than it is for technology and 
manufacturing firms.

Employment and Human Resources 
The Trump administration’s policies on China have also not had a major effect on general employment and 
hiring practices in the financial industry. Overall, between January 2017 and March 2020, total employment 
in the U.S. financial industry increased every month, from 8.4 million jobs to 8.8 million jobs—until the 
start of the global COVID-19 pandemic, when 261,000 jobs were lost in April 2020, according to the BLS.
105 
Employment has since rebounded month over month. 
Like other industries, however, the tightening immigration regime in the United States under the 
Trump administration arguably made it more difficult for financial companies to hire Chinese nationals 
domestically—but this problem appears less germane for U.S. firms in finance, than, say, high-tech firms in 
Silicon Valley.
106 Indeed, in our interviews during the fall of 2020, U.S. financial sector executives did not 
report any notable disruptions to their ability to hire and maintain talent domestically as a result of tighter 
immigration restrictions
107—despite the fact that, industry-wide, the rejection rate for high-skilled workers 
attempting to obtain H-1B visas rose under the Trump administration from 6.1 percent in 2016 to 15.1 per -
cent in 2019.108
Part of this is because of the way financial companies have adapted already. American financial compa -
nies that are expanding their operations in China increasingly rely on local talent, mainly because of Chi -
na’s increasing pool of young, highly skilled professionals who know the domestic market. China’s financial 
opening has likely resulted in further localization of human resources, at least marginally, amid rising geo -
political tensions between the two countries. Discussions with contacts in leading financial institutions sug -
gested that increasing emphasis on local hiring is part of a deep localization strategy that could insulate com-
panies in times of geopolitical tensions.
109 For example, Goldman Sachs’s five-year strategic plan included 
doubling its personnel in China, but finding, competing for, and compensating the right local talent may 
be challenging.
110 As another example, S&P Global has chosen to hire the majority of its employees locally, 
while bringing in more experienced employees who are native Mandarin speakers from other offices outside 
China. Likewise, to break into the Chinese life insurance subsector, some major U.S. insurers have adapted 
their U.S. business models to focus on retail-based sales of life policies with an entirely domestic Chinese 
agent force and telephone sales team.
111 
Multiple U.S. industry executives interviewed for this study, however, expressed concerns about their abil-
ity to retain highly skilled, multilingual young professionals in China. According to them, local talent are at 
risk of “being poached” by or “jump[ing] ship” for competitive opportunities with domestic and state-owned 
financial firms.
112 
105 BLS, 2021a. 
106 Michelle Hackman, “Trump Moves to Temporarily Suspend New H-1B, Other Visas amid Covid-19 Pandemic,” Wall 
Street Journal , June 23, 2020.

November 5, 2020 (names withheld on request).

Views from the Financial Sector
95
Changes Since the Onset of COVID-19 
In the short term, the most immediate consequence of the COVID-19 pandemic on the financial industry’s 
core business activities in China was a deepening of digital capabilities, including both virtual sales and 
essential client services, such as document signatures, billing, and claims.
113 Indeed, industry leaders believed 
that these adaptations in virtual consumer connections will have an enduring effect on the financial services 
industry’s business operations in China. Anecdotally, for example, financial officers cited their expanded 
reliance on the WeChat platform for both back-of-the house operations and client interface throughout the 
pandemic.
114 
One industry representative also reflected that a significant, enduring consequence of the pandemic for 
financial firms—like those in tech and manufacturing—will be the realization that “resilience is now worth a 
cost.” Whereas for tech and manufacturing firms, creating resilience might be an exercise in building supply 
chain redundancies, in the case of financial firms, developments in resilience are more likely to take the form 
of distributed computer networks.
115
For the U.S. financial industry’s longer-term interests in China, the most direct effect of the COVID-19 
pandemic may be a flood of nonperforming loans for Chinese banks, particularly from corporate borrowers 
(including SOEs), as the banks continue to “clean up their balance sheets and offload risky assets” that partly 
fueled the unprecedented banking industry expansion of the last decade discussed previously.
116 In the short 
term, the PRC government put in place loan forbearance programs, as well as new preferential lending pro -
grams for firms determined as essential to the pandemic response. But to attract new foreign capital into the 
banking system over the long run, the Chinese government may eventually need to increase foreign investor 
confidence in the financial system with a bail out of domestic banks—namely, by directly buying COVID-
related nonperforming loans off the balance sheets.
117 
Less directly, and over longer time horizons, the pandemic’s enduring effect on the operations of U.S. 
financial firms in China will be mainly a function of consequences for GDP growth and a positive interest 
rate. Both factors played in China’s favor in late 2020 given the forecasted pace of economic recovery and the 
zero-rate policies in Europe and the United States. While there are many reasons to doubt the accuracy of 
China’s official GDP statistics, most forecasts from international institutions and major financial firms pre -
dicted a faster economic recovery in China relative to most advanced and developing economies.
118 In areas 
like insurance and personal wealth management, one crosscurrent to macroeconomic slowdown may be a 
decline in risk tolerance for uncertainty—and an increase in demand for these financial products for those 
who can afford them.
119 
113 Telephone interview with industry group representative, September 22, 2020 (name withheld on request).
114 Telephone interviews with industry group representatives, September 22, 2020; October 9, 2020 (names withheld on 
request).
115 Telephone interview with industry group representative, November 5, 2020 (name withheld on request).

Bridging the Gap
96
Responses to Shape U.S. Policies or PRC Responses
Overall, U.S. financial industry leaders interviewed expressed neither approval for nor deep anxieties about 
the prospect of tougher U.S. economic policies leading to harmful restrictions on operations or transactions 
with Chinese firms or individuals, at least not in the near- or medium-term future. As one interviewee noted, 
when most American firms (i.e., in tech and manufacturing) approach the U.S. government to shape poli -
cies toward China, they generally are seeking substantive help with enforcement issues or protection from IP 
violations—but less so financial firms.
120 
In general, leaders of the U.S. financial industry have remained relatively reticent in expressing their criti-
cisms of recent U.S. government policy actions toward the PRC as a result of escalating U.S.-China competi -
tion. Indeed, in interviews with the RAND research team, some industry insiders explained that the Trump 
administration’s perceived tendency to “name and shame” U.S. business leaders and firms has deterred more 
vocal opposition to White House trade policies in public forums.
121 
Instead, in RAND interviews, some industry insiders explained that the escalating trade war (and other 
U.S. policy actions) have driven corporate leaders in the U.S. financial industry to take a more active role in 
serving in leading advisory boards and business councils as a preferred channel to conduct advocacy activi -
ties. One interviewee noted that this practice was viewed as providing shelter from potentially retaliatory 
Trump administration rhetoric by aggregating private-sector perspectives on a particular issue.
122
U.S. financial industry leaders interviewed also acknowledged that direct engagement remained an 
important way to express policy positions and assert a seat at the table—namely, through the access, influ -
ence, and communication channels of high-level individuals and Washington lobbyists. 123 However, one 
business community leader expressed the feeling that many career officials at agencies such as the DOC and 
the USTR had been pushed out of the policymaking process during the Trump administration, sometimes 
harming financial giants’ ability to influence the White House’s U.S.-China economic initiatives through 
typical input channels with the professional staff of the relevant federal agencies.
124
Overall Effect of Increased U.S.-China Competition on the Sector 
Generally, the U.S. financial sector has been unenthusiastic about most punitive measures resulting from 
increased U.S.-China competition, such as tariffs, financial sanctions, and restrictions on foreign talent. 
These reactions stem from a set of structural features of the U.S. financial industry, including its domi -
nant position in the global market, long positions in assets and global diversification, strong belief in capital 
mobility, and aversion to uncertainty and market volatility.
The dominant position of U.S. financial institutions means that they do not view Chinese firms as seri -
ous competitors in the global market. For the most part, U.S. finance majors compete with each other and, 
increasingly, with other U.S. technology companies. They view China as a market and a business opportu -
nity, not as a competitor. U.S. financial firms are generally further removed from the nexus with national 
120 Telephone interview with industry group representative, November 5, 2020 (name withheld on request).
121 Telephone interviews with industry group representatives, September 22, 2020; and October 22, 2020 (names withheld on 
request). This was similarly echoed “Is Wall Street Winning in China?” 2020. 
122 Telephone interviews with industry group representatives, September 22, 2020; October 22, 2020; and November 5, 2020 
(names withheld on request).
123 Telephone interviews with industry group representatives, October 22, 2020; October 30, 2020; and November 5, 2020 
(names withheld on request).
124 Telephone interview with industry group representative, November 5, 2020 (name withheld on request).

===== PDF_PAGE 111 =====
Views from the Financial Sector
97
security than are many tech and manufacturing companies. A long position in assets means that the industry 
is focused on growth, both in the United States and globally, including in the Chinese market. The global 
exposure of American finance majors implies that they may suffer from the second-order consequences of 
the U.S.-China competition, such as the potential deceleration in other emerging economies due to a poten -
tial slowdown in the Chinese economic growth due to trade wars, technology restrictions, and fallout from 
the global pandemic. There are certain exceptions, as some hedge funds may thrive on disruption while trad-
ing platforms may see increased volume in times of tensions, but overall the focus remains on the long-term 
growth of assets and geographic diversification. The latter also implies that China’s economic performance 
will likely lead to increased allocations of U.S. capital to the Chinese equity and bond markets, and, con -
versely, of Chinese capital to Western markets. 
Overall, despite increased policy uncertainty and market volatility induced by the trade war—as well as 
tightening security legislation in mainland China and in Hong Kong—the Trump administration’s policies 
toward China have had a neutral effect on the U.S. financial sector (see Table 8.2). Although China increased 
market access for asset managers, banks, insurance firms, and other financial services firms and credit rating 
agencies, it is debatable whether China did so as a result of pressure from the Trump administration or 
out of its own interest. Industry insiders believe that the Trump administration at most accelerated China’s 
timeline for market reforms. Other actions by the administration did help U.S. companies—for example, in 
U.S. domestic markets, escalatory policy retaliations have encouraged increased transparency and regulatory 
oversight of Chinese firms publicly traded on American exchanges.

CHAPTER NINE
Conclusions and Implications for the 
U.S. Government
The U.S. legal and regulatory environment has been an important factor in U.S. business success. Laws 
enable the enforcement of contracts, the protection of IP, and the raising of capital. Ultimately, the U.S. busi-
ness community will follow whatever laws and regulations the U.S. government institutes regarding doing 
business with China, but there are three notable cautions. First, U.S. businesses will certainly try to find legal 
workarounds, at times staying within the letter of the law rather than the spirit. Second, U.S. laws regarding 
doing business with China could disadvantage U.S. businesses in relation to foreign competitors, which may 
not have the same restrictions, with the result that only the U.S. businesses are hurt and policy goals regard -
ing China are not met. Third, as noted in Chapter Eight, there is ambiguity about how the new U.S. measures 
will be applied, and this could lead to lower levels of business activity out of caution. This ambiguity would 
compound the ambiguity that China has already created through the way it applies its laws and regulations. 
Because of these three cautions, there is value to having a stronger understanding of how U.S. businesses have 
viewed and adapted to new U.S. measures regarding China. 
Business Community Support for U.S. Policies Toward China
The U.S. business community broadly agreed with the Trump administration’s identification of problematic 
Chinese behavior and practices. These concerns included PRC government support for Chinese companies 
that gave them an unfair advantage in China and globally; barriers to obtaining licenses and approvals for 
operations in China; barriers to investing in China; uneven enforcement of PRC regulations and laws or an 
uncertain PRC policy climate; lack of IP rights enforcement or protection for operations in China; lacking 
cybersecurity and technology security for operations in China, including negative consequences of China’s 
cyberregulation and data regulation for business operations; and restrictive PRC capital controls. We found 
this support across all three sectors and across U.S. firms with different market capitalization and different 
customer bases (whether domestic and international). 
It is important to note that despite such concerns over unfair PRC government actions and regarding 
operations in China, many business leaders we interviewed continued to see the large China market as an 
opportunity and their companies sought to expand business or operations in China or continue to engage in 
transactions with Chinese companies. This aligns with our national survey of U.S. individuals from March 
to April 2020, in which we found 49 percent of all respondents viewed China as an economic opportunity, 
compared with 36 percent that viewed it as an economic threat and 15 percent that viewed it as neither an 
economic threat nor opportunity. Our study thus found only limited support from the U.S. business com -
munity for a broad decoupling between the U.S. and Chinese economies. 
The U.S. business community, however, had concerns with some of the specific approaches and measures 
the administration embraced to address the situation. In the manufacturing and technology sectors, U.S. 
corporate leaders felt that unilateral tariffs and restrictions on the types of activities and transactions U.S.

===== PDF_PAGE 114 =====
Bridging the Gap
100
companies could conduct with Chinese actors or in China placed the companies at a disadvantage and did 
not significantly change or modify PRC behavior. Many corporate leaders voiced concern that the tariffs that 
the United States levied on Chinese products hurt U.S. businesses more than Chinese ones. 
In particular, there was very little support for Section 301 tariffs. Industry groups and specific companies 
noted the need for the United States to embrace a more balanced approach toward China that incorporates 
“both carrots and sticks, leverage and direct negotiation, existing trade enforcement tools and new ones 
crafted to China’s market-distorting behaviors.”
1 The Motor & Equipment Manufacturers Association, for 
example, has recommended that the United States combine more targeted PRC policies with “sustained bilat-
eral negotiations with China that pocket progress as it occurs, as well as multilateral negotiations and pres-
sure in concert with USMCA, EU, Japan, and other allies.”
2 A number of corporate leaders we interviewed 
voiced concern that the tariffs encouraged Chinese companies to switch to working with European or Japa-
nese firms and did not change PRC government support for Chinese companies.
Corporate leaders across all three sectors further expressed frustration at what they perceived to be the 
inconsistent way in which the Trump administration consulted U.S. businesses for input and information 
before rolling out major policy changes and reforms. To some, the administration appeared to consult com-
panies and corporate leaders that were more supportive of the president’s overall political platform and 
agenda. To others, some of the administration’s economic policy and actions toward China were relatively 
difficult to predict and did not seem to follow known timelines. Coupled with the uncertainty associated 
with the COVID-19 pandemic, the administration’s actions undermined their firms’ ability to engage in 
long-term corporate planning. One executive, for example, mentioned that he could only plan for the next 
three to four months. 
Of the three sectors examined in this report, the financial sector had more positive assessment of the 
Trump administration’s China-related actions than the other sectors. Figure 9.1 shows how S&P 500 compa-

101
nies viewed their China-related operations through coding and averaging what individual companies stated 
in the transcripts of their quarterly earnings calls. A higher average sentiment score captures greater opti -
mism and positive assessments of the firms’ China-related operations or activities. As is shown, the average 
sentiment score for U.S. manufacturing and technology dropped from 2017 to mid-2020. In contrast, the 
average sentiment score for U.S. financial companies had a bit more volatility in late 2017 and early 2018 
(both spiking and dipping) before rising. Financial companies had a Q2 2020 average sentiment score that 
was higher than their Q1 2017 score. 
The financial sector also benefited the most from the Trump administration’s China-related economic 
policies. As summarized in Table 9.1, the overall effect of the Trump administration’s actions toward China 
was neutral for financials. This contrasts with the overall negative consequences the manufacturing and 
technology sectors experienced from 2017 to 2020. 
Part of why the financial sector fared the best is because the Trump administration did not view deeper 
U.S. and Chinese financial integration as a major threat to U.S. national security and interests compared with 
the threat it assessed from China on the manufacturing and technology fronts—whether, for example, in the 
form of concerns about U.S. manufacturers being able to compete with Chinese companies or PRC theft or 
acquisition of U.S. IP rights or dual-use technology.
Instead, during the Trump administration, U.S. financial firms obtained more access to the PRC financial 
market. It is unclear if the administration’s policies caused China to accelerate liberalization of its financial 
markets or if China was already on track to do so due to internal PRC considerations. Chinese policymakers 
may have sought to open their financial markets to leverage foreign expertise to professionalize the sector 
and deal with the range of problems the current PRC financial sector faces, including nonperforming loans. 
Beijing appears to have also timed its financial market liberalization until after key Chinese companies, such 
as the e-commerce companies like Ant Financial, Alibaba, and Tencent, have established significant market 
dominance. 
The financial sector also had less to lose. Relative to the manufacturing and technology sectors, U.S. 
financial firms listed in the S&P 500 index are significantly less dependent on revenues from China. Nearly 
six in ten U.S. financial firms listed in the index had no reported global sales to the PRC in 2019—including 
100 percent of regional banks, 58 percent of asset managers and banks, 52 percent of insurance companies, 
and 38 percent of diversified finance firms. This share is in marked contrast to the manufacturing and tech -
nology sectors, where only 21 percent and 13 percent of S&P 500 firms, respectively, reported no global sales 
to China. China is also currently a modest destination of FDI from the U.S. financial sector ($14.6 billion)—
slightly more so than from the U.S. technology sector ($12.6 billion), but significantly less so than from the 
U.S. manufacturing sector ($43.8 billion).

Bridging the Gap
102
Looking beyond the economic considerations driving the Trump administration’s economic measures 
against China, we found mixed support from the U.S. business community on whether the United States 
should use economic sanctions to encourage China to change its geopolitical positions or practices. A few 
U.S. corporate leaders we interviewed expressed strong views that corporations should be willing to support 
overall U.S. government policy even if it meant that, at least in the short term, U.S. companies may have to 
suffer economic losses. Others, however, pointed out that using economic sanctions for political purposes 
risks generating techno-nationalism not only in China but globally. This could have significant negative 
consequences for larger U.S. firms serving an international market. It is possible that consequences could be 
mitigated if other major advanced economies joined in the sanctions, but the people we interviewed did not 
raise this issue, nor did we pursue it in our discussions. Our survey results of U.S. individuals also portray 
a split view on this topic, with more individuals in favor of supporting U.S. policy at an economic cost: we 
found that 60 percent of individual respondents were in favor of U.S. companies not selling goods and ser -
vices to Chinese actors that undermine U.S. interests or values even if it weakens the U.S. economy. Forty 
percent did not support such limitations. 
Our survey results also found that the U.S. government may not always need to take action against prob -
lematic PRC products or actors—the U.S. public (and consumers) may avoid buying PRC products if they are 
informed of the risks. We asked U.S. individuals if they would be willing to buy a Huawei telephone given 
that the U.S. government has said that Huawei, a Chinese telecommunications company, could be collecting 
user information and intelligence on behalf of the Chinese military (Huawei has disputed such allegations) 
and many countries use Huawei phones. We found that 83 percent of respondents were not willing to buy the 
Chinese phone.
Recommendations for the U.S. Government 
In formulating any policy toward China, it is important to recognize that China is intertwined with the U.S. 
and global economies and that China is a large economy and getting larger. The United States has limited 
leverage on its own, and any policy is likely to have costs. While the Trump administration’s policies can be 
criticized, coming up with effective alternatives is not a simple task. With that in mind, our research suggests 
a number of recommendations for how the Biden administration can improve its ability to work with the U.S. 
business community to advance U.S. economic interests and counter problematic PRC economic behavior: 
•
 Ad
opt an economic strategy toward China that balances efforts to counter China with investments 
in strengthening and sustaining U.S. economic and technological leadership. The Trump admin -
istration’s economic approach to compete with China was largely focused on countering China via 
imposing unilateral tariffs on Chinese trade, preventing Chinese control of sensitive U.S. technology, 
and limiting transfers of U.S. technology to China. Although the administration did seek to invest 
domestically to strengthen U.S. technological leadership, it decided to increase investment in select 
areas (e.g., AI and quantum physics) and not in basic science. There are a large number of measures 
the Biden administration could embrace to help the United States and U.S. companies compete with 
China. In general terms, the administration could increasing funding for science, technology, engineer-
ing, and mathematics education and research or provide tax incentives for companies to invest more 
in R&D. More specifically, U.S. policy development could be directed toward providing new methods 
of skill building beyond traditional educational programs; explore programs to improve labor market 
matching to make better use of human capital; and create programs or facilities, such as shared design 
or production centers, to accelerate the diffusion of advanced manufacturing techniques.

===== PDF_PAGE 117 =====
Conclusions and Implications for the U.S. Government
103
• Embrace the full range of trade and economic tools to counter China and expand those tools. The 
n
ew administration could also do more to employ a fuller range of tools to counter problematic PRC 
behavior. For example, the United States can work to restore the dispute resolution system in the WTO 
and more aggressively pursue cases there. The United States currently seizes counterfeit and pirated 
goods and has a process to block imports of items with stolen IP; this could be broadened to block any 
import from a company using stolen IP. Other policies could include measures involving reciprocal 
treatment for market access and more aggressively limiting Chinese investment or ownership in the 
United States. Aside from capital returns to recipients of investment, it is not clear that Chinese invest -
ment brings the most important benefits of FDI, such as new management methods and markets. In 
combination, these tools could help U.S. companies compete on a more level playing field and obtain 
more market access in China without necessarily requiring U.S. companies to incur the costs of tariffs 
or limits on their activities in China. 
•
 Wo
rk with allies and partners to embrace multilateral measures against problematic PRC activi-
ties. The Biden administration should consult allies and partners prior to embracing major economic 
actions and, if possible, adopt multilateral instead of unilateral economic actions. Unilateral U.S. politi-
cal or economic pressure against problematic PRC economic behavior is unlikely to change China’s 
behavior and may hurt U.S. businesses instead of benefiting them. Unilateral restrictions on economic 
activity with China also risk larger geopolitical ramifications beyond the U.S.-China relationship and 
could influence other countries to adopt similar protectionist measures to protect their manufacturing 
or technology sectors. This could challenge existing global economic rules that support an open market 
and free trade. Although the U.S. economy is larger than the Chinese economy, the U.S. economy com-
bined with the economies of its EU, G7, and other allies is far larger still, and even more important to 
the Chinese economy; the EU and the United States are the dominant import markets for Chinese busi-
nesses, and China relies on those sales for employment and growth. 
•
 Re
gularly consult U.S. business leaders in advance of major policy changes. U.S. business leaders have 
significant information and experience dealing with the Chinese government and competing against 
Chinese firms, and they can provide valuable input into potential major economic U.S. policy changes 
or actions targeting China. According to our interviews, this was one of the biggest gaps in Trump 
administration policy implementation. Even if U.S. corporate leaders disagree with a proposed policy 
change, consultations and advance notice will allow them to better plan and adapt business operations 
accordingly. Furthermore, greater consultation could make them less likely to resist such policies.
•
 Av
oid U.S. government actions that target the Chinese people. There is little public support in the 
United States for limiting PRC nationals from studying or working in the United States. Chinese stu -
dents and workers also contribute significantly to the U.S. economy and U.S. companies as valuable 
colleagues and business leaders.
•
 Ad
opt an information strategy to better inform the public about the risks of or problems associated 
with PRC products. Our survey results showed that, when informed of the potential risks of a Chinese 
phone, a majority of the U.S. public declined to purchase the phone. This suggests that the United States 
could invest in efforts to publicize information regarding the operations and relationships of problem -
atic Chinese companies and allow U.S. customers make informed decisions on what products to pur -
chase. These information activities could be conducted in place of or in conjunction with U.S. govern -
ment measures to limit the activities of select PRC companies.

APPENDIX A
RAND American Life Panel China Survey Results
This appendix provides additional information related to the nationally representative survey conducted 
from the beginning of March 2020 through the end of April 2020, as discussed in Chapter Four. Here we pro-
vide information on the mapping of occupations to industries and full statistical results.
Occupations and Industries
This report focuses on how specific industries have responded to U.S. China-related policies. Yet the RAND 
ALP does not report an industry variable. It does, however, ask for a respondent’s occupation, for which the 
possibilities are listed in the “ALP Occupation” column in Table A.1. To better relate the survey results to our 
industry analysis, we assigned each ALP occupation into one of four different industries: financials, technol -
ogy, manufacturing, and other.
The survey data included occupational variables (which we concorded to industries), demographic vari -
ables, geographic variables, and political variables. We provide summary statistics in Table A.2.
Tables A.3–A.4 provide full regression results for our analysis of the four survey questions. These tables 
provide both the coefficients from the regressions as well as the average marginal effects.
