APPENDIX E
Validation of Categorization of Earnings Statements
Chapter Five uses FinBERT to code as positive, neutral, or negative 21,492 sentences from S&P 500 earnings 
calls and 6,151 sentences from Russell 2000 earnings calls. Each of these sentences contained one of the four 
keywords or variants on which we searched:
•
 Chin
a, including Beijing, China, Chinese, and Shanghai
• ge
opolitical, including geopolitical, geo-political, and geopolitics
• tari
ff, including tariff and tariffs 
• tra
de war, including trade tension and trade war.
In addition to the verbal description of positive, neutral, or negative, FinBERT delivered a numerical score 
from 1.0 to –1.0. We used the numerical score in our analysis (averaging scores per quarter), so the verbal coding 
is less important. Nonetheless, the verbal coding provides a useful way for us to validate FinBERT’s rating.
Relying on FinBERT’s natural language–processing model to conduct sentiment analysis has the benefit 
of lower human error in coding, but the automated system could make errors as well. Three types of errors 
present themselves.
First, the sentiment could be incorrectly coded: a negative sentiment could be coded as positive, and vice 
versa. Second, a sentiment could be off-topic despite the keyword. As a hypothetical example, a statement 
such as “My birthday cake was delicious and served on fine china” could be rated as a positive sentiment 
related to China. Third, the strength of the sentiment could be incorrectly rated. 
We opted to cross-check the ratings for the first two errors because whether the coding was right or wrong 
would be more obvious for these two errors, whereas more judgment would be needed—and larger differ -
ences of opinion could emerge—regarding the third error. To do so, two RAND researchers independently 
assessed 10 percent of all S&P 500 sentences and 10 percent of all Russell 2000 sentences. 
We selected sentences to be checked by using Excel’s random number generator. For the S&P 500 sen -
tences, we generated 2,149 random numbers, and for the Russell 2000 sentences, we generated 615 random 
numbers. Each person who validated the ratings was given a different list of random numbers, so at most we 
checked 4,298 unique S&P 500 sentences and 1,230 unique Russell 2000 sentences, although it is likely we 
checked fewer. The main difficulty in reviewing the sentences was that they were presented without context.
Reviewer Results
Reviewer One
Our first reviewer found that of the 2,149 S&P 500 sentences reviewed,
• 11
2 were ambiguous
• 31 w
ere clearly misclassified
• fo
ur were not on topic.

Bridging the Gap
136
This means that 147 sentences out of 2,149, or 6.8 percent, were incorrectly classified.
Of 615 Russell 2000 sentences reviewed,
• 38
 were ambiguous
• 14 w
ere clearly misclassified
• fiv
e were not on topic.
This means that 57 sentences out of 615, or 9.3 percent, were incorrectly classified. 
Reviewer Two
Our second reviewer used a different rating system and reviewed 2,150 S&P 500 sentences and 615 Russell 
2000 sentences. We opted to have the raters work independently to see whether the analysis stood up under 
different methods of evaluation.
Among the 791 positive S&P 500 sentences in the sample,
•
 69
2, or 87.5 percent, were coded correctly
• 36
, or 4.6 percent, were coded incorrectly
• 50
, or 6.3 percent, were ambiguous
• 13
, or 1.6 percent, were not on topic.
Among the 310 negative S&P 500 sentences in the sample,
• 25
5, or 82.3 percent, were coded correctly
• 16
, or 5.2 percent, were coded incorrectly
• 28
, or 9.0 percent, were ambiguous
• 11
, or 3.5 percent, were not on topic.
In addition, 1,048 sentences in the sample were coded as neutral. We discuss neutral statements below.

, or 5.5 percent, were ambiguous

• 72

• tw

In addition, 325 sentences in the sample were coded as neutral. We discuss neutral statements below, 
along with a number of other issues that may suggest weaknesses in our results.

===== PDF_PAGE 151 =====
Validation of Categorization of Earnings Statements
137
Discussion of Results
Beyond simply assessing the correctness of ratings, there are a number of other factors important to evaluat -
ing the machine-generated results. 
Neutral Sentences
We did not use the positive, neutral, or negative ratings in our analysis. Rather, we used the numerical score 
for each sentence, and averaged them to assess the trend. In discussing these trends, we noted when numbers 
were positive or negative and considered that to be the indicator of sentiment.
However, the sentences designated as neutral by FinBERT could be a problem if they were negative but 
labeled with a positive number, or positive but labeled with a negative number. In the S&P 500 data set, there 
were 10,799 sentences labeled as neutral, ranging from 0.49 to –0.45. In the Russell 2000 data set, there were 
3,250 such sentences, ranging from 0.49 to –0.43.
We took a more detailed look at neutral statements in the Russell 2000 data set. There are clear examples 
of statements that lean positive, and have negative numerical ratings, and statements that lean negative, and 
have positive numerical ratings.
For example, this statement received a score of 0.345 but is clearly on the negative side: But when you have 
a 25% tariff, you can’t mitigate that completely, you need to pass on price increases. 
While the software performed well at the tails, it is not clear that it performed as well the closer the scores 
moved toward zero. We cannot assess the effect of this on our analysis. If the errors were not systematic, then 
they should average out and have little effect on our conclusions. However, if positive sentences were consis-
tently given negative scores or vice versa, our analysis may be in error.
Challenges with Clearly Positive and Negative Sentences
Although sentences rated as positive were generally positive, and those rated as negative were generally nega-
tive, there is one caveat. Statements coded as positive do not necessarily mean they were supportive of the 
trade war policies, although many were. Instead, sentences coded as positive sentiment often involved firms 
encouraging their investors things were going well despite the U.S. policies toward China. This does not cause 
problems for our analysis because our goal was to assess sentiment toward business with China rather than 
sentiment toward U.S. policies. 
Complexity and Miscoding
Although few sentences with a clear negative or positive sentiments were blatantly miscoded, miscoded sen -
tences tended to be more complex sentences or have more complex relationships between positive and nega-
tive words and meanings. 
For example, this risk management sentiment was given a numerical score of –0.944, but it could be inter-
preted as positive: We saw our risk management metrics in full action in August when markets fell nearly 2% 
on tariff concerns, and the strategy outperformed its benchmark.
Likewise, this sentence was coded as positive, and was in fact overall positive, but sentiment toward China 
is negative: From a regional perspective, we saw strong growth over market in all major regions of the world 
despite weakness in Europe and China markets.
Complex sentences often had several sentiments, and these sometimes resulted in miscoding as well. 
For example, the following sentence was coded as negative, but the net sentiment is neither clearly positive 
nor negative, although it could be interpreted as leaning positive: In the atmosphere of the kind of the macro

===== PDF_PAGE 152 =====
Bridging the Gap
138
uncertainty driven by trade war rhetoric and so on and so forth and the impacts of the tariffs, I’d say, generally 
speaking, customers are remaining optimistic although there is, obviously, concern which is dampening enthu-
siasm for laying down CapEx and taking a long-term view to demand.
And in some cases, technical issues resulted in an incorrect score. For example, the software did not 
understand that lower freight rates are good. This sentence received –0.932: As we look at freight rates, clearly, 
you guys highlighted, obviously, we’re heading into the Chinese New Year, rates have come down.
Challenges with Questions
The earnings call transcripts also contain questions from those attending the meeting. The software rated 
these as well, although they do not necessarily reflect the sentiment of the company. There is no simple way 
to exclude them from the analysis.

167
About the Authors
Bonny Lin is a political scientist at the RAND Corporation. Her research focuses on U.S. defense policy 
toward China, India, and the Indo-Pacific region. Lin holds a Ph.D. in political science.
Howard J. Shatz is a senior economist at the RAND Corporation. He specializes in international eco -
nomics and economics and national security. Shatz holds a Ph.D. in public policy.
Nathan Chandler is a senior policy analyst at the RAND Corporation. His research focuses on a diverse 
range of U.S. defense and international security policy issues, including strategic competition, military inter-
ventions, and the defense industrial base. Chandler holds an MPP in international and global affairs. 
Cristina L. Garafola is an associate policy researcher at the RAND Corporation. Her research focuses 
on the implications of Chinese foreign and defense policy for the United States and the Indo-Pacific region. 
Garafola holds an M.A. in international relations. 
Eugeniu Han is a doctoral fellow at the RAND Corporation. He does research at the intersection of inter-
national economics and national security with a regional focus on China and Russia. 
Andy Law is currently a second-year law student. He is interested in international economics and eco -
nomics and national security. Law holds a B.S. in economics.
King Mallory is a senior international defense researcher at the RAND Corporation. His research focuses 
on great power competition, alliance networks, burdensharing, counterproliferation, and deterrence.
Zev Winkelman is a senior information scientist at the RAND Corporation specializing in big data ana -
lytics, social media, and cybersecurity. Winkelman holds a Ph.D. in public policy.

===== PDF_PAGE 182 =====
$49.00
T
he administration of President Donald Trump pursued an enhanced—and, 
in some ways, novel—set of policies designed to confront China over its 
transgressive and anticompetitive economic behaviors, such as theft of 
technology and intellectual property and limitations on market access 
for U.S. businesses. How did the U.S. business community view these 
policies, and did it broadly support increased U.S. efforts to counter problematic Chinese 
economic behavior? If not, how could the U.S. government implement policy to better 
achieve policy goals while also addressing corporate concerns? 
In this report, the authors address these questions, which are central to determining 
whether the U.S. government has crafted an overall economic strategy or approach 
toward China that is sustainable and feasible. How the business community thinks the 
United States should deal with China is an overlooked and underappreciated topic, and 
the United States might find it difficult to compete against China without support from the 
business community.
The authors assess how the U.S. business community—focusing on the manufacturing, 
technology, and financial sectors—viewed the Trump administration’s China policies 
through several different lenses: from the perspective of individuals, from the perspective 
of different firms over time, and from the perspective of specific industry sectors and 
subsectors. The analysis focuses on Trump administration actions toward China from 
2017 through fall 2020.
